# Vladimir Putin, Russian Federation, 2023

## 1. Overview

Vladimir Putin was President of the Russian Federation throughout 2023 and remained the decisive actor in foreign policy, military strategy, security policy, senior appointments, and the legislative agenda. His formal authority was extensive, but implementation depended on the security services, military command, regional administrations, courts, state-owned enterprises, and politically connected economic actors. The June 2023 Wagner mutiny demonstrated that coercive authority was not perfectly unified: Yevgeny Prigozhin’s forces advanced toward Moscow before a negotiated withdrawal.

The period was dominated by the continuing full-scale war against Ukraine, intensified domestic repression, military mobilization and war spending, efforts to preserve economic stability under sanctions, and preparation for the 2024 presidential election. Information was difficult to assess because Russian authorities criminalized independent war reporting, restricted civil society, controlled major media, and reduced transparency around military casualties and wartime spending.

## 2. Evidence records

### Record VP-2023-01 — Putin’s formal office and concentration of executive authority

Putin held the office of President of the Russian Federation in 2023. The presidency controlled the armed forces, foreign policy, senior security appointments, and the issuance of binding decrees. The legislature and courts remained formally separate institutions, but available comparative assessments describe Russia as an authoritarian system with severely restricted political competition and judicial independence.

This matters because attribution of national security, repression, and strategic policy decisions to Putin is strong, although many operational acts were carried out by subordinate ministries, security agencies, regional officials, and military commanders.

- Strongest source: Freedom House, “Russia: Freedom in the World 2023,” Freedom House, 2023, country assessment sections on political pluralism, executive dominance, speech, and rule of law. [Direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/russia/freedom-world/2023)
- Period: 2023, with some inherited institutional conditions.
- Attribution: Strong for presidential direction and appointments; weaker for individual abuses not directly documented as presidential orders.
- Complicating evidence: Formal constitutional institutions, elections, courts, and parliament continued to operate and sometimes affected implementation.
- Cautions: Freedom House is an advocacy-oriented comparative evaluator; its ratings should be combined with official records, court materials, and independent reporting.
- Independent support: V-Dem, BTI, Freedom House Nations in Transit, and the 2023 OVD-Info review.

### Record VP-2023-02 — Continuation and escalation of the war against Ukraine

Russia continued its full-scale invasion of Ukraine throughout 2023. The United Nations Commission of Inquiry reported that Russian authorities committed widespread violations of international humanitarian and human-rights law, including attacks affecting civilians and infrastructure, torture, and unlawful transfers and deportations. Putin’s direct responsibility is supported by his position as commander-in-chief and by the centralized political control of the war, though responsibility for particular incidents requires incident-specific evidence.

- Strongest source: UN Commission of Inquiry on Ukraine, “War crimes, indiscriminate attacks on infrastructure, systematic and widespread torture show disregard for civilians,” United Nations, 16 March 2023. [Direct link](https://ukraine.un.org/en/223552-war-crimes-indiscriminate-attacks-infrastructure-systematic-and-widespread-torture-show)
- Locator: Commission findings summarized in the report announcement, especially the sections on attacks, torture, and transfers.
- Period: Mainly 2023, while describing conduct beginning in 2022.
- Attribution: Strong for continuation of the war and strategic policy; variable for individual battlefield incidents.
- Complicating evidence: Russia denied many allegations and presented its military actions as lawful and defensive.
- Cautions: Access to occupied areas was restricted; wartime investigations often rely on interviews, satellite evidence, open-source material, and evidence collected by one side.
- Independent support: UN General Assembly Resolution ES-11/6, ICC Ukraine situation materials, Carnegie Endowment analyses, and high-quality war reporting.

### Record VP-2023-03 — Wagner mutiny exposed limits within the coercive apparatus

On 23–24 June 2023, Wagner forces led by Prigozhin seized or approached key military facilities and advanced toward Moscow. The mutiny ended after negotiations involving Belarusian leader Alexander Lukashenko. The episode exposed conflict between Wagner and the Ministry of Defence and showed that Putin’s system depended on competing coercive organizations whose loyalty could not be assumed automatically.

Putin retained power and subsequently reasserted control, but the episode was a significant test of regime effectiveness and military command cohesion.

- Strongest source: Associated Press, “Russian Mercenary Boss Yevgeny Prigozhin Challenged the Kremlin in a Brief Mutiny,” Associated Press, 2023. [Direct link](https://apnews.com/article/0c0d4e6619ef9cb89df6a45a431ff53c)
- Period: 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to Putin’s crisis management and command structure; indirect for the original institutional conflict.
- Complicating evidence: The mutiny ended without civil war, and the federal state ultimately preserved control.
- Cautions: The negotiated terms, internal elite bargaining, and military readiness remain partly opaque.
- Independent support: Carnegie Endowment, “Band-Aid Politics: How the Kremlin Is Dealing With the Fallout from Prigozhin’s Mutiny.” [Direct link](https://carnegieendowment.org/russia-eurasia/politika/2023/06/band-aid-politics-how-the-kremlin-is-dealing-with-the-fallout-from-prigozhins-mutiny)

### Record VP-2023-04 — ICC arrest warrant concerning Ukrainian children

In March 2023, the International Criminal Court issued an arrest warrant for Putin concerning alleged unlawful deportation and transfer of Ukrainian children from occupied Ukrainian territory to Russia. The ICC stated that there were reasonable grounds to believe Putin bore individual responsibility, including through subordinates and failure to exercise proper control.

The warrant is a major legal and reputational consequence of policies involving occupied Ukrainian territory and children.

- Strongest source: International Criminal Court, “Situation in Ukraine,” ICC, 2023–present. [Direct link](https://www.icc-cpi.int/situations/ukraine)
- Locator: ICC description of the arrest warrant and alleged responsibility under Articles 8 and 25/28 of the Rome Statute.
- Period: Conduct beginning before and continuing into 2023; warrant issued in 2023.
- Attribution: Strong legally as an allegation supported by the Court’s “reasonable grounds” standard; not a final conviction.
- Complicating evidence: Russia rejected the Court’s jurisdiction and allegations; access to records and affected children was contested.
- Cautions: An arrest warrant is not a final judgment. Quantifying the full population of transferred children remains difficult.
- Independent support: UN Commission of Inquiry, UN Security Council briefing on Ukrainian children, and UNICEF-related reporting.

### Record VP-2023-05 — Wartime repression and criminalization of dissent

Independent rights monitor OVD-Info recorded 2,830 administrative cases under the law against “discrediting” the armed forces in 2023, and 794 people in anti-war criminal cases by December, compared with 378 at the end of 2022. It reported that average prison terms in anti-war cases increased, while journalists, activists, lawyers, and opposition figures faced prosecution, detention, censorship, and restrictions on legal access.

The evidence indicates a shift from mass protest policing toward selective, intimidating prosecution and self-censorship.

- Strongest source: OVD-Info, “Repressions in Russia in 2023: Review,” OVD-Info, 18 December 2023. [Direct link](https://data.ovd.info/repressii-v-rossii-v-2023-godu-obzor-ovd-info)
- Locator: Sections on anti-war administrative cases, criminal cases, journalists, detention conditions, and repressive legislation.
- Period: 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to state policy and the legal-security apparatus; direct personal attribution to Putin is strongest where laws or policies were presidentially signed or publicly endorsed.
- Complicating evidence: Authorities described prosecutions as enforcement against extremism, terrorism, or false information.
- Cautions: OVD-Info is a rights-monitoring organization operating under pressure; its figures are documented minimums and likely undercount coercion that victims do not report.
- Independent support: Freedom House, Committee to Protect Journalists, UN special-procedure materials, and Russian court records.

### Record VP-2023-06 — Expansion of wartime administrative and military controls

In 2023, Russia expanded coercive legal powers connected to the war. OVD-Info documented legislation introducing electronic military summonses and a unified military-registration system, restrictions on people who ignored summonses, harsher penalties for sabotage and treason, and broader application of laws against discrediting the armed forces.

These measures increased the state’s ability to mobilize personnel, restrict movement and property transactions, and punish dissent.

- Strongest source: OVD-Info, “Repressions in Russia in 2023: Review,” OVD-Info, 18 December 2023. [Direct link](https://data.ovd.info/repressii-v-rossii-v-2023-godu-obzor-ovd-info)
- Locator: Sections on the April electronic-summons law and 2023 amendments to treason, sabotage, extremism, and military-discrediting provisions.
- Period: 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for the national legislative program; implementation varied by region and agency.
- Complicating evidence: The government presented the measures as administrative modernization and national security policy.
- Cautions: The review summarizes legislation and enforcement from a rights perspective; official legal texts should be used for final legal interpretation.
- Independent support: Official Russian legal database, State Duma legislative records, Kremlin acts archive.

### Record VP-2023-07 — Economic resilience under sanctions accompanied by inflation and structural strain

Russia avoided the severe economic collapse expected by some observers after the invasion and sanctions. The World Bank described 2023 activity as a softer contraction or rebound relative to earlier expectations, while warning about inflation, tighter financial conditions, and the continuing effects of the invasion. The Bank of Russia’s 2023 annual report documents extensive monetary and financial-policy intervention.

The result was short-term macroeconomic resilience, but with increased dependence on wartime expenditure, state direction, sanctions adaptation, and non-Western trade channels. Nominal output measures should not be treated as evidence of improved living standards without real-income, regional, and distributional data.

- Strongest source: World Bank, “Russia’s Invasion of Ukraine and Cost-of-Living Crisis Dim Growth Prospects in Emerging Europe and Central Asia,” World Bank, 6 April 2023. [Direct link](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/04/06/russian-invasion-of-ukraine-and-cost-of-living-crisis-dim-growth-prospects-in-emerging-europe-and-central-asia)
- Corroborating official source: Bank of Russia, “Annual Report for 2023,” Bank of Russia, 2024. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Period: 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for the government’s macroeconomic response and war-spending priorities; weaker for household-level outcomes.
- Complicating evidence: Official Russian data and the central bank reported stabilization and adaptation; some sectors and households benefited from defense-related demand.
- Cautions: Wartime statistical revisions, restricted disclosure, exchange-rate effects, and military production complicate comparisons.
- Independent support: Associated Press, “Russia’s Economy Holds Up, but Growing Challenges Test Putin”; World Bank 2024 regional update; Bank of Russia monetary-policy reports.

### Record VP-2023-08 — Nuclear signaling and erosion of arms-control constraints

Putin continued to use nuclear signaling in the context of the Ukraine war. Russia suspended participation in New START in February 2023 and announced plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in Belarus. These actions increased strategic risk and weakened remaining US–Russia arms-control constraints, although suspension did not immediately equal formal withdrawal from every treaty obligation.

- Strongest source: Associated Press, “Russia Suspends Only Remaining Major Nuclear Treaty with US,” Associated Press, 2023. [Direct link](https://apnews.com/article/23cc21a1f42798177a3f4)
- Corroborating source: United Nations Security Council meeting record on nuclear risk and Ukraine. [Direct link](https://digitallibrary.un.org/nanna/record/4008427/files/S_PV.9300-EN.pdf)
- Period: 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for presidential nuclear rhetoric and treaty policy.
- Complicating evidence: Russian officials framed the measures as responses to NATO policy and Western support for Ukraine; no nuclear weapon was used.
- Cautions: Public statements do not reveal operational readiness or actual deployment status.
- Independent support: AP reporting on Belarus, UNODA treaty materials, Federation of American Scientists nuclear estimates.

### Record VP-2023-09 — Political competition and civic space remained severely restricted

Russia’s 2023 political environment provided limited meaningful competition. Freedom House reported that vague extremism laws, restrictions on speech and organization, post-mobilization controls, and pressure on independent groups sharply constrained political participation. The 2023 regional and local elections occurred in a wartime environment in which opposition organizations, independent media, and prominent critics had been weakened or excluded.

- Strongest source: Freedom House, “Russia: Freedom in the World 2023,” Freedom House, 2023. [Direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/russia/freedom-world/2023)
- Period: Conditions inherited from 2022 and continuing through 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for the legal and institutional environment; electoral claims require election-specific documentation.
- Complicating evidence: Elections were held, parties operated, and authorities retained formal electoral procedures.
- Cautions: Freedom House’s aggregate rating does not substitute for precinct-level election observation.
- Independent support: Freedom House Nations in Transit 2023, Golos reporting, OSCE archives, CPJ Russia archive.

## 3. Information environment and limitations

Reliable information was difficult to obtain for several reasons:

- Russian censorship and criminal penalties discouraged reporting on military failures, casualties, and civilian harm.
- Independent media outlets were blocked, forced abroad, labeled “foreign agents,” or designated extremist.
- Political opponents, lawyers, journalists, and rights monitors faced detention, prosecution, surveillance, or exile.
- Military, defense-industrial, and budget information was selectively withheld or revised.
- Official Russian statistics remain useful for some macroeconomic indicators but are difficult to interpret where wartime production, exchange rates, sanctions, and regional inequality affect the data.
- Ukrainian, Russian opposition, Western-government, and international-organization sources each have institutional or political perspectives.
- Access to occupied Ukrainian territory and detained persons was highly restricted.
- International investigations often provide stronger evidence of patterns than of complete totals or individual command chains.

The strongest approach is triangulation: official legal and economic records, UN and ICC investigations, independent Russian monitoring, international statistical sources, and incident-level reporting.

## 4. Questions for deeper research

1. What documentary evidence connects Putin personally to specific military targeting, deportation, filtration, or torture policies?
2. How did the Kremlin resolve the Wagner crisis inside the military, intelligence services, and regional governments?
3. What were the real regional and household effects of inflation, labor shortages, mobilization, and defense spending?
4. How competitive were the 2023 regional elections in practice, including candidate exclusion and administrative pressure?
5. What independent evidence exists on deaths, disappearances, and mistreatment in Russian detention facilities?
6. How much did sanctions alter Russia’s long-term productivity, technology access, and fiscal sustainability?
7. Which anti-corruption or audit findings implicated officials close to Putin, and what accountability followed?
8. What limits did China, Turkey, Belarus, Central Asian governments, and Russian economic elites place on Putin’s choices?

- The official Russian legal texts for the 2023 electronic-summons and expanded military-censorship laws.
- The Federation Council’s conclusion on execution of the 2023 federal budget.
- The 2023 OVD-Info thematic reports on anti-war repression and journalist prosecutions.

--- RESEARCH HANDOFF ---

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

# Nuclear and Existential-Risk Responsibility — Vladimir Putin, Russia, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Putin was President and commander-in-chief throughout 2023. Russia’s presidential system gave him decisive authority over nuclear doctrine, military strategy, foreign policy, senior security appointments, and treaty decisions. The formal chain also included the Security Council, Defence Ministry, General Staff, Strategic Rocket Forces, Rosatom, the Federal Assembly, and diplomatic agencies. Practical constraints included military-bureaucratic implementation, elite bargaining, Belarusian dependence, NATO and US deterrence, sanctions, and the possibility of escalation beyond Moscow’s control.

The inherited baseline was a large, modernizing Russian nuclear arsenal; extensive strategic and non-strategic forces; established command-and-control institutions; and a deteriorating arms-control relationship with the United States. Russia’s full-scale war against Ukraine created the dominant 2023 risk environment. The Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant, occupied by Russia, became an additional nuclear-safety concern.

The clearest adverse developments were Putin’s suspension of New START participation, loss of treaty verification, the announcement of tactical nuclear deployment in Belarus, and Russia’s withdrawal of CTBT ratification. Putin’s rhetoric repeatedly framed nuclear capability as a response to Western escalation and used strategic ambiguity to deter outside intervention. There is no public evidence that Russia used a nuclear weapon or that an accidental nuclear launch occurred in 2023, but absence of an accident is not evidence that safeguards were effective.

Information quality was mixed. Treaty texts, presidential decrees, UN and IAEA records, and official statements are strong for formal acts and stated policy. Nuclear-force numbers, readiness, deployment, command safeguards, and accident-prevention procedures remain partly classified. Russian and Western claims about Belarusian deployment and nuclear signaling conflict. Access to Zaporizhzhia and occupied territories was limited.

## 2. Evidence index

### WEB-1B-01 — New START suspension and loss of verification

On 21 February 2023 Putin announced that Russia would “suspend” participation in New START. The treaty did not contain a general suspension mechanism, and Russia subsequently stopped data exchanges, inspections, and related transparency measures. The Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists assessed that there was no indication Russia had exceeded the treaty’s central numerical limits, but that loss of verification would reduce confidence in the status of Russian forces.

- Source: *2024 Doomsday Clock Statement: Nuclear Risk*, Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, 23 January 2024. [Direct link](https://thebulletin.org/2024-statement/nuclear-risk)
- Locator: “An undiminished nuclear threat and a new arms race,” paragraphs on New START.
- Status: Later retrospective evidence about a 2023 act.
- Attribution: Direct presidential decision, with implementation by the Russian government and military.
- Contrary evidence: Russia presented the suspension as a response to US/NATO policy and stated that it remained within treaty limits.
- Cautions: The Bulletin is an expert policy publication, not a treaty-monitoring body; its assessment depends partly on publicly available information.
- Independent corroboration: UN Security Council discussions, US State Department treaty statements, Arms Control Association reporting.

Supports: 1B.1, 1B.2, 1B.4, 1B.8, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-02 — Tactical nuclear weapons announced for Belarus

In March 2023 Putin announced that Russia would deploy tactical nuclear weapons in Belarus. The Bulletin reported that it remained unclear whether weapons had actually been moved by the end of the year. Russia’s UN mission stated that Russia retained “full control” over the weapons and characterized the arrangement as deterrence and retaliation against NATO.

- Strongest source: *2024 Doomsday Clock Statement: Nuclear Risk*, Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, 23 January 2024. [Direct link](https://thebulletin.org/2024-statement/nuclear-risk)
- Official source: Russian Mission to the UN, “Right of Reply … First Committee,” 5 October 2023. [Direct link](https://russiaun.ru/en/news/1051023)
- Locator: Bulletin paragraphs on Belarusian deployment; Russian statement paragraphs 37–48.
- Status: 2023 target-period evidence, with retrospective technical assessment.
- Attribution: Directly attributable to Putin’s public decision and Russian-Belarusian state policy.
- Contrary evidence: Belarus and Russia described the arrangement as defensive and retaliatory; the exact deployment status was disputed.
- Cautions: Official Russian claims are self-interested; independent verification of warhead movement was unavailable.
- Independent corroboration: AP, BBC, TIME, UN First Committee materials.

Supports: 1B.1, 1B.2, 1B.5, 1B.8, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-03 — Withdrawal of CTBT ratification

In October 2023 Russia’s State Duma voted to withdraw Moscow’s ratification of the Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty. Russia remained a CTBT signatory, and Putin stated that Russia would not resume testing unless the United States did so. The change nevertheless weakened the legal and political barrier against renewed testing.

- Source: *2024 Doomsday Clock Statement: Nuclear Risk*, Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, 23 January 2024. [Direct link](https://thebulletin.org/2024-statement/nuclear-risk)
- Locator: Paragraph on the October 2023 Duma vote and Putin’s testing statement.
- Status: Later retrospective evidence concerning a 2023 formal act.
- Attribution: Strong: the Duma acted legislatively and Putin signed the resulting measure.
- Contrary evidence: Russia retained its signature and publicly maintained a conditional testing moratorium.
- Cautions: Withdrawal of ratification did not itself prove that testing was planned or imminent.
- Independent corroboration: CTBTO statements, Russian legal records, UN First Committee debate.

Supports: 1B.1, 1B.2, 1B.4, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-04 — Nuclear rhetoric and war-related escalation risk

During 2023 Russia presented nuclear policy as a response to Western support for Ukraine and the risk of “strategic defeat.” The Russian UN mission described nuclear cooperation with Belarus as retaliatory and argued that NATO nuclear sharing created the relevant escalation. The Bulletin recorded continuing concern about possible Russian nuclear use in the Ukraine conflict.

The evidence supports a finding of coercive and escalatory nuclear signaling. It does not establish that Putin intended to use nuclear weapons or that a launch order was issued.

- Sources: Russian Mission to the UN, 5 October 2023. [Direct link](https://russiaun.ru/en/news/1051023); Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, 23 January 2024. [Direct link](https://thebulletin.org/2024-statement/nuclear-risk)
- Locator: Russian statement paragraphs 37–48; Bulletin nuclear-risk paragraphs 127–132.
- Status: Target-year rhetoric, with later retrospective assessment.
- Attribution: Strong for official Russian messaging and presidential nuclear decisions.
- Contrary evidence: Russian officials repeatedly described the posture as deterrence rather than preparation for nuclear use; no nuclear weapon was used.
- Cautions: Public rhetoric is strategic communication and cannot by itself reveal operational intent.
- Independent corroboration: Putin’s Kremlin speeches, AP, UN Security Council records, SIPRI and FAS assessments.

Supports: 1B.2, 1B.5, 1B.8.

### WEB-1B-05 — Nuclear safety risks at Zaporizhzhia

The Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant remained under Russian occupation and military control during 2023. IAEA reporting emphasized the need to maintain nuclear safety, security, staffing, logistics, external power, and communication. The plant was not destroyed and no radiological release occurred, but the war created conditions in which shelling, military presence, staffing pressure, and loss of normal regulatory arrangements increased accident risk.

- Source: IAEA, *Nuclear Safety, Security and Safeguards in Ukraine: February 2022–February 2023*. [Direct link](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/02/nuclear-safety-security-and-safeguards-in-ukraine-feb-2023.pdf)
- Related source: UN Security Council coverage of IAEA safety principles. [Direct link](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sc15300.doc.htm)
- Locator: IAEA sections on Zaporizhzhia’s external power, staffing, military activity, and safeguards.
- Status: 2023 target-period evidence, with inherited conditions from 2022.
- Attribution: Strong for Russia’s occupation and military presence; individual incidents require separate attribution.
- Contrary evidence: IAEA access and continued operation reduced some immediate risks; Russia permitted limited IAEA presence.
- Cautions: IAEA access was constrained and the agency generally avoids assigning legal responsibility for military conduct.
- Independent corroboration: UN Security Council records, Ukrainian regulator statements, satellite and open-source analysis.

Supports: 1B.3, 1B.5, 1B.8, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-06 — Nuclear arsenal and force posture

The supplied local briefing identified FAS nuclear estimates from 2014, including approximately 4,300 military-stockpile warheads and approximately 8,000 total warheads. These are inherited baseline facts, not evidence of a 2023 change. The 2023 FAS narrative source was not accessible in this pass, so current force totals, modernization progress, readiness, and tactical-warhead deployment require further inspection.

- Baseline source candidate: Federation of American Scientists, *Russian Nuclear Forces 2023*. [Link](https://fas.org/publication/russian-nuclear-forces-2023)
- Status: Inherited baseline; 2023 technical update not fully extracted.
- Attribution: Arsenal size is a national capability, not by itself a ruler-quality finding. Putin’s attribution is stronger for doctrine, modernization priorities, and deployment decisions.
- Contrary evidence: Public estimates differ by counting method and classified inventories.
- Cautions: FAS estimates are expert estimates, not official inventories.
- Independent corroboration: SIPRI Yearbook, Bulletin Nuclear Notebook, IISS Military Balance.

Supports: 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-07 — Formal foreign-policy doctrine claimed peace and strategic stability

Putin signed Presidential Decree No. 229 approving the 2023 Foreign Policy Concept. The document described Russia as a major nuclear power with responsibility for global balance, emphasized strategic stability, international law, diplomacy, and prevention of global conflict, while also presenting confrontation with the West as central to Russian policy.

- Source: Presidential Decree No. 229, *Concept of the Foreign Policy of the Russian Federation*, Russian Gazette, 31 March 2023. [Direct link](https://rg.ru/documents/2023/03/31/prezident-ukaz229-site-dok.html)
- Locator: Decree paragraphs 54–66; Concept paragraphs 85–89, 197–217.
- Status: 2023 target-period formal act.
- Attribution: Direct presidential act.
- Contrary evidence: The same document legitimized forceful opposition to Western influence and was issued while Russia continued the war against Ukraine.
- Cautions: Strategic concepts describe official policy, not necessarily implementation or actual restraint.
- Independent corroboration: Kremlin archive, Carnegie, ECFR, UN records.

Supports: 1B.1, 1B.2, 1B.4, 1B.5, 1B.8.

### WEB-1B-08 — No evidence of independent expert correction or transparent review

The inspected sources did not establish that Putin created or empowered independent experts with protected authority to challenge nuclear signaling, arms-control withdrawal, Belarusian deployment, or wartime nuclear-safety risks. Public evidence instead shows decisions communicated through presidential, military, diplomatic, and state-media channels. This is a research gap rather than proof that no internal safeguards existed.

- Evidence basis: inspected 2023 Foreign Policy Concept, Russian UN statement, Bulletin assessment, IAEA materials, and UN records.
- Status: Target-period gap.
- Attribution: Institutional attribution is tentative; classified command arrangements may not be visible publicly.
- Cautions: Nuclear command structures are inherently secret, and public absence of evidence cannot prove absence of expert dissent.
- Independent corroboration needed: biographies and appointment records for the Security Council, General Staff, Strategic Rocket Forces, Rosatom, and nuclear-safety regulators; parliamentary hearings; declassified intelligence; technical audits.

Supports: 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.8, 1B.9.

### WEB-1B-09 — Other catastrophic risks: no developed 2023 record

The candidate catalogue included academic work on AI risk, but those sources were not directly tied to Putin’s 2023 policy, resources, appointments, safeguards, or implementation. The 2023 Foreign Policy Concept mentioned AI, biological technologies, information technologies, and dangerous pathogens as transnational risks, but this is only a formal acknowledgment, not evidence of effective safeguards.

- Source: Presidential Decree No. 229, 31 March 2023. [Direct link](https://rg.ru/documents/2023/03/31/prezident-ukaz229-site-dok.html)
- Locator: Concept paragraph 7 on emerging technologies and paragraph 9 on transnational threats.
- Status: Target-period contextual evidence.
- Attribution: Strong for formal recognition; insufficient for implementation.
- Cautions: No inspected evidence established budgets, independent review, enforcement, or correction mechanisms for AI, cyber, biological, or dual-use risks.
- Further research: Russian AI strategy implementation, cyber incident investigations, biological-laboratory governance, Rosatom dual-use controls, and independent technical institutions.

Supports: 1B.9.

## 4. Source groups

### Fully opened and retained

1. Presidential Decree No. 229 / 2023 Foreign Policy Concept — Russian Gazette.
2. Russian Mission to the UN statement on nuclear cooperation with Belarus.
3. Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, 2024 nuclear-risk retrospective.
4. IAEA Ukraine nuclear-safety report.
5. UN Security Council materials on IAEA nuclear-safety principles.
6. Carnegie Endowment analysis of the Wagner crisis and Russian wartime policy.
7. ECFR analyses of Russia’s wartime foreign policy.
8. UN and ICC Ukraine materials previously opened in the orientation pass.
9. Bank of Russia Annual Report 2023, for broader war-state resource context.

### Opened mainly for corroboration

- Associated Press reports on New START and Belarus.
- UN First Committee and Security Council nuclear-risk coverage.
- China’s 2023 UN First Committee statement on strategic-risk reduction. [Direct link](https://un.china-mission.gov.cn/eng/chinaandun/disarmament_armscontrol/unga/202310/t20231017_11162037.htm)
- Carnegie Endowment reports on Russian wartime foreign policy.
- ECFR reports on Russia’s relations with Iran and the West.

### Unopened or access-blocked leads

- FAS, *Russian Nuclear Forces 2023*. [Link](https://fas.org/publication/russian-nuclear-forces-2023)
- CTBTO official 2023 materials on Russian ratification withdrawal and test-site monitoring.
- UNODA treaty records on New START and CTBT.
- UN Security Council documents S/2023/393 and S/PV.9300.
- Russian official legal database for the CTBT withdrawal statute.
- Russian nuclear doctrine and military-strategy documents.
- SIPRI and FAS technical force estimates.
- Bulletin Nuclear Notebook country assessment.
- Academic sources on AI and catastrophic risk.
- Detailed IAEA follow-up reports after February 2023.
- Official and independent records concerning Belarusian warhead deployment.

At least 12 catalogue candidates were inspected or attempted. Several UN, AP, FAS, and IAEA URLs were inaccessible or timed out through the browsing layer; the accessible sources yielded repeated evidence on arms-control erosion, Belarusian deployment, rhetoric, and Zaporizhzhia safety.

## 5. Remaining research gaps

1. Verify the exact legal instrument and date of Russia’s CTBT ratification withdrawal.
2. Establish whether tactical nuclear warheads physically entered Belarus during 2023.
3. Obtain 2023 FAS/SIPRI estimates and distinguish deployed, stored, retired, and reserve warheads.
4. Inspect official and independent evidence on nuclear command-and-control safeguards.
5. Identify any internal military, scientific, or Security Council dissent concerning escalation.
6. Determine whether Russia maintained or weakened nuclear incident hotlines and crisis communications.
7. Inspect Russia’s nonproliferation controls involving Iran, North Korea, Belarus, private firms, and illicit procurement networks.
8. Develop a separate record on cyber, biological, AI, and other dual-use catastrophic-risk governance.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

# International Peace, Restraint, and Lawful Security — Vladimir Putin, Russia, 2023

## 1. Orientation

The conflict-exposure gate is clearly met. Putin was President and commander-in-chief throughout 2023, with decisive authority over war aims, military policy, foreign relations, treaty positions, senior security appointments, and the use of state resources. Russia’s Federal Assembly, Defence Ministry, General Staff, security services, regional authorities, and state-controlled media implemented policy, but none provided a reliable independent check on presidential war decisions.

The inherited condition was Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine, begun in February 2022. In 2023 Putin’s government continued offensive operations, occupied Ukrainian territory, supported regular and irregular forces, used long-range strikes, rejected the UN framework for ending the war, and pursued territorial claims. The principal external constraints were Ukrainian resistance, Western military support and sanctions, NATO deterrence, economic limitations, battlefield losses, and the risk of escalation with other nuclear powers.

The strongest evidence shows that peaceful alternatives remained available in the form of diplomacy, ceasefire proposals, prisoner exchanges, humanitarian arrangements, and negotiations, but that Russia did not make a durable settlement its central policy. International organizations documented civilian harm, unlawful attacks, torture, forced transfers, and difficulties obtaining access to prisoners and occupied areas. Russia denied many allegations and framed its war as defensive and necessary to protect Russian security.

## 2. Compact evidence index

### WEB-2B-01 — Continued war and rejection of the UN settlement framework

Russia continued its full-scale war in 2023 despite the UN General Assembly’s February 2023 resolution calling for principles consistent with the UN Charter, including respect for Ukraine’s sovereignty and territorial integrity and a just and lasting peace.

- Source: UN General Assembly Resolution ES-11/6, 23 February 2023. [Direct link](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4004933)
- Locator: Resolution title, operative paragraphs concerning sovereignty, withdrawal, peace, and compliance with the Charter.
- Period: Target-year 2023; inherited war began in 2022.
- Attribution: Strong for Putin’s government because continuation of the war and territorial policy were presidential and national-security decisions.
- Contrary evidence: Russia argued that Western governments had rejected Russia’s security concerns and that negotiations required recognition of Russian territorial claims.
- Cautions: A General Assembly resolution establishes an international political and legal position, not a judicial finding of individual criminal responsibility.
- Independent corroboration: Carnegie Endowment, UN Commission of Inquiry, OHCHR, UCDP/PA-X records, and Putin’s own public statements.

Supports: 2B.1, 2B.2, 2B.3, 2B.7, 2B.10.

### WEB-2B-02 — Civilian casualties and Russian long-range attacks

OHCHR documented 25,170 civilian casualties in Ukraine between 24 February 2022 and 30 June 2023: 9,177 killed and 15,993 injured. In its report covering February–July 2023, OHCHR stated that Russian forces launched 94 percent of the recorded missile, air-strike, and loitering-munition attacks in the relevant dataset, many affecting densely populated areas away from the frontline.

- Source: OHCHR, *Report on the Human Rights Situation in Ukraine, 1 February to 31 July 2023*, 4 October 2023. [Direct PDF](https://www.ohchr.org/sites/default/files/documents/hrbodies/hrcouncil/coiukraine/23-10-04-OHCHR-36th-periodic-report-ukraine-en.pdf)
- Locator: Paragraphs 28–32, especially civilian-casualty totals and missile/air-strike findings.
- Period: Target period 2023, with cumulative figures from 2022.
- Attribution: Strong for the recorded attacks attributed to Russian armed forces; attribution of every individual strike remains incident-specific.
- Contrary evidence: Russia claimed that strikes targeted military or strategic objects and denied deliberate attacks on civilians.
- Cautions: OHCHR lacked full access to occupied territory and stated that actual casualty figures were likely higher.
- Independent corroboration: UN Commission of Inquiry, OHCHR monthly reports, satellite/open-source investigations, and Ukrainian civil-defense records.

Supports: 2B.2, 2B.3, 2B.4, 2B.6, 2B.9.

### WEB-2B-03 — UN finding of indiscriminate Russian attacks

The UN Commission of Inquiry found reasonable grounds to conclude that Russian armed forces carried out indiscriminate attacks affecting a residential building in Uman and civilian sites in Kherson in April–May 2023. The Commission concluded that Russian forces failed to take feasible precautions to ensure that affected objects were not civilian.

- Source: UN Commission of Inquiry on Ukraine, *Report of the Independent International Commission of Inquiry on Ukraine*, 2023. [Direct PDF](https://www.ohchr.org/sites/default/files/documents/hrbodies/hrcouncil/coiukraine/A-78-540-En.pdf)
- Locator: Paragraphs 18–19 and footnote defining “Russian authorities.”
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to Russian armed forces; the source does not establish that Putin personally selected each target.
- Contrary evidence: Russia disputed UN findings and maintained that its attacks were lawful military operations.
- Cautions: The Commission used a “reasonable grounds” standard, not the proof standard of a criminal conviction.
- Independent corroboration: OHCHR, Human Rights Watch, Amnesty International, satellite imagery, and local reporting.

Supports: 2B.2, 2B.3, 2B.4, 2B.6, 2B.9.

### WEB-2B-04 — Deportation and transfer of Ukrainian children

The ICC issued an arrest warrant for Putin alleging responsibility for the war crimes of unlawful deportation and transfer of Ukrainian children from occupied areas to Russia. The Court stated that there were reasonable grounds to believe Putin bore responsibility directly, jointly, through others, or through failure to exercise proper control over subordinates.

- Source: International Criminal Court, *Situation in Ukraine*, 2023. [Direct link](https://www.icc-cpi.int/situations/ukraine)
- Locator: Suspect profile for Vladimir Putin; alleged crimes and Articles 25(3) and 28(b) responsibility basis.
- Period: Conduct began in 2022 and continued into the target period.
- Attribution: Strong legal attribution at the preliminary-warrant stage.
- Contrary evidence: Russia rejected ICC jurisdiction and characterized transfers as humanitarian evacuations or protection of children.
- Cautions: An arrest warrant is not a final judgment; the allegation remains subject to trial-level proof.
- Independent corroboration: UN Commission of Inquiry, UN Committee on the Rights of the Child, AP reporting, Ukrainian tracing and reunification records.

Supports: 2B.2, 2B.4, 2B.5, 2B.9.

### WEB-2B-05 — Prisoners of war and limited humanitarian access

The ICRC reported continuing work in the Russia–Ukraine international armed conflict and stated that it had visited more than 1,500 prisoners of war on both sides since February 2022. Its Olenivka material also recorded that repeated requests for access to the facility and affected prisoners had not produced the access required for a full assessment.

- Source: ICRC, *Russia–Ukraine international armed conflict: ICRC continues to help people in need*, 2023. [Direct link](https://www.icrc.org/en/document/russia-ukraine-international-armed-conflict-icrc-continues-to-help-people-in-need)
- Related source: ICRC, *Olenivka penal facility: Prisoners of war and ICRC’s role*, updated 26 July 2023. [Direct link](https://www.icrc.org/en/document/olenivka-penal-facility-prisoners-war-and-icrcs-role)
- Locator: ICRC figures on POW visits and sections describing requests for access to Olenivka.
- Period: Target period 2023, with inherited detention issues from 2022.
- Attribution: Strong for Russia’s obligations and practical control over Russian-held facilities; weaker for particular deaths or abuses without access.
- Contrary evidence: ICRC continued to conduct some visits and humanitarian work.
- Cautions: The ICRC deliberately limits public attribution and did not independently determine responsibility for all incidents.
- Independent corroboration: OHCHR thematic POW report, UN Commission of Inquiry, ICC materials, and prisoner testimony.

Supports: 2B.4, 2B.5, 2B.6, 2B.9.

### WEB-2B-06 — Limited independent scrutiny and information restrictions

Russia’s wartime legal and media environment restricted independent examination of military conduct. OVD-Info recorded criminal and administrative cases for anti-war speech, while Freedom House described severe restrictions on independent media, political competition, and civil society. These restrictions reduced the ability of journalists, parliamentarians, courts, investigators, and victims to test official claims about military necessity and civilian harm.

- Sources: OVD-Info, *Repressions in Russia in 2023: Review*, 18 December 2023. [Direct link](https://data.ovd.info/repressii-v-rossii-v-2023-godu-obzor-ovd-info); Freedom House, *Russia: Freedom in the World 2023*. [Direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/russia/freedom-world/2023)
- Locator: OVD-Info sections on anti-war criminal cases, journalists, and repressive legislation; Freedom House sections on speech, media, political pluralism, and rule of law.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for government-created legal and institutional constraints.
- Contrary evidence: Formal courts, parliament, and official investigation procedures continued to exist.
- Cautions: OVD-Info is an independent rights monitor whose figures are minimum documented cases; Freedom House is a comparative advocacy evaluator.
- Independent corroboration: CPJ, UN special procedures, Russian court records, and exile-media investigations.

Supports: 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.9.

### WEB-2B-07 — Wagner mutiny and proxy-force control

The June 2023 Wagner mutiny exposed the limits of Kremlin control over a heavily armed proxy organization. Wagner forces seized military facilities and advanced toward Moscow before a negotiated withdrawal. The episode showed that Russia’s use of proxy forces created internal command, accountability, and escalation risks, even though Putin ultimately restored formal control.

- Source: Carnegie Endowment, “Band-Aid Politics: How the Kremlin Is Dealing With the Fallout from Prigozhin’s Mutiny,” 2023. [Direct link](https://carnegieendowment.org/russia-eurasia/politika/2023/06/band-aid-politics-how-the-kremlin-is-dealing-with-the-fallout-from-prigozhins-mutiny)
- Locator: Analysis of the June mutiny, Kremlin response, and relationship between Wagner and the Defence Ministry.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for Putin’s appointment and tolerance of the proxy structure; indirect for Prigozhin’s independent decisions.
- Contrary evidence: The state ultimately prevented the mutiny from becoming a civil war and reabsorbed or dismantled much of Wagner’s domestic role.
- Cautions: Elite bargaining and private security arrangements were partly opaque.
- Independent corroboration: AP, ECFR, Carnegie’s broader wartime-policy analyses, and official Kremlin statements.

Supports: 2B.5, 2B.8, 2B.9, 2B.10.

### WEB-2B-08 — Military resources and war-finance priorities

The local briefing identified SIPRI 2023 military expenditure for Russia at approximately $109 billion in constant 2024 dollars and approximately $750 per capita. These figures show the scale of resources committed to military power, but they do not by themselves establish whether spending was proportionate, corruption-free, or directed toward genuine defensive needs.

The Bank of Russia and World Bank evidence indicates that Russia preserved short-term economic stability while redirecting resources toward wartime production and state demand.

- Sources: SIPRI military-expenditure data in the local package; Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf); World Bank, 2023 regional update. [Direct link](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/04/06/russian-invasion-of-ukraine-and-cost-of-living-crisis-dim-growth-prospects-in-emerging-europe-and-central-asia)
- Locator: SIPRI 2023 Russia expenditure observations; Bank of Russia sections on fiscal, financial, and wartime economic conditions.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for national budgetary and military priorities; weak for individual procurement decisions.
- Contrary evidence: The government characterized spending as necessary national defense and economic stabilization.
- Cautions: Military budgets are partly classified and nominal/real exchange-rate comparisons are difficult.
- Independent corroboration: SIPRI, Russian budget documents, Accounts Chamber, IISS, and World Bank.

Supports: 2B.2, 2B.5, 2B.8, 2B.10.

### WEB-2B-09 — Official justification and stated peaceful alternatives

Putin’s government publicly justified the war through claims about NATO expansion, protection of Russian-speaking populations, alleged Ukrainian threats, and the need to prevent a strategic defeat of Russia. The 2023 Foreign Policy Concept simultaneously emphasized diplomacy, international law, and peaceful dispute resolution while describing the West as an existential strategic adversary.

The record establishes the content of the justification, but available evidence does not show that Russian decision-makers were presented publicly with a balanced assessment of alternatives or that official claims were subject to independent review.

- Source: Presidential Decree No. 229, *Concept of the Foreign Policy of the Russian Federation*, 31 March 2023. [Direct link](https://rg.ru/documents/2023/03/31/prezident-ukaz229-site-dok.html)
- Locator: Concept paragraphs 85–99, 197–217.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Direct presidential doctrine and official representation.
- Contrary evidence: Russia argued that negotiations and settlement were impossible without recognition of Russian security interests and territorial claims.
- Cautions: A strategy document records official framing but does not independently verify the factual premises.
- Independent corroboration: Kremlin speeches, Carnegie, ECFR, UN General Assembly records, and international legal assessments.

Supports: 2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.7.

### WEB-2B-10 — Absence of durable ceasefire or lawful settlement

No durable Russia–Ukraine ceasefire or peace settlement was achieved in 2023. Russia continued military operations, maintained territorial claims, and rejected settlement terms centered on Ukrainian sovereignty and withdrawal. Humanitarian arrangements, prisoner exchanges, and the Black Sea Grain Initiative were partial and fragile rather than a comprehensive settlement.

- Sources: UN General Assembly Resolution ES-11/6. [Direct link](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4004933); Carnegie, *Russia’s War in Ukraine: One Year On*. [Direct link](https://carnegieendowment.org/research/2023/02/russias-war-in-ukraine-one-year-on); ECFR, *The Art of Vassalisation*. [Direct link](https://ecfr.eu/publication/the-art-of-vassalisation-how-russias-war-on-ukraine-has-transformed-transatlantic-relations)
- Locator: UN resolution operative provisions; Carnegie and ECFR sections on war aims, negotiations, and regional consequences.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for Russia’s continuing war policy and negotiating position.
- Contrary evidence: Russia participated in prisoner exchanges, humanitarian measures, and some diplomatic contacts.
- Cautions: Negotiation records are incomplete and both sides used public diplomacy strategically.
- Independent corroboration: UN, ICRC, OHCHR, Turkish mediation records, and reputable reporting.

Supports: 2B.1, 2B.2, 2B.7, 2B.10.

## 3. Lens dispositions

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 2B.1 Peaceful alternatives | Adverse evidence developed | WEB-2B-01, 09, 10 |
| 2B.2 Starting or prolonging war | Strong adverse record | WEB-2B-01, 02, 03, 04, 10 |
| 2B.3 Honest justification for force | Official justifications documented; accuracy disputed | WEB-2B-02, 03, 06, 09 |
| 2B.4 Civilian and prisoner protection | Strong adverse and incomplete record | WEB-2B-02, 03, 04, 05 |
| 2B.5 Control of proxies and arms | Mixed but materially adverse | WEB-2B-04, 05, 07 |
| 2B.6 Scrutiny of war claims | Strong adverse evidence | WEB-2B-03, 06, 09 |
| 2B.7 Ceasefires and settlements | Partial humanitarian measures, no durable settlement | WEB-2B-05, 09, 10 |
| 2B.8 Responsible military resources | Large wartime resource commitment documented; proportionality and corruption require more work | WEB-2B-07, 08 |
| 2B.9 Accountability and remedy | Strong accountability gap | WEB-2B-04, 05, 06 |
| 2B.10 Lasting international peace | Adverse end-state evidence; longer-term outcome remains open | WEB-2B-01, 07, 08, 10 |

## 4. Information environment and limitations

Evidence is strongest for:

- formal Russian decrees and public statements;
- UN resolutions and reports;
- documented attacks in government-controlled territory;
- ICC allegations and arrest-warrant reasoning;
- ICRC access and humanitarian activity;
- restrictions on independent scrutiny.

Evidence is weaker for:

- Russian military casualties and battlefield decisions;
- classified targeting orders and rules of engagement;
- treatment of prisoners in inaccessible facilities;
- proxy-force financing and command arrangements;
- internal Russian deliberations over negotiations;
- corruption and patronage within military procurement;
- harm in occupied territory where international monitors lacked access.

The principal source limitations are wartime propaganda, censorship and intimidation in Russia, restricted access to occupied areas, incomplete casualty verification, strategic use of public diplomacy by all belligerents, and the difference between international legal findings, preliminary allegations, and final criminal judgments.

## 5. Source groups

### Fully opened and retained

- UN General Assembly Resolution ES-11/6.
- OHCHR 36th periodic report on Ukraine.
- OHCHR civilian-casualty reports for 2023.
- UN Commission of Inquiry report.
- ICC Ukraine situation page.
- ICRC Russia–Ukraine conflict material.
- ICRC Olenivka material.
- OVD-Info 2023 repression review.
- Freedom House Russia 2023.
- Carnegie Endowment analyses.
- ECFR policy analyses.
- Russian 2023 Foreign Policy Concept.
- Bank of Russia Annual Report 2023.
- World Bank 2023 economic update.
- Local SIPRI military-expenditure observations.

### Opened mainly for corroboration

- Associated Press Russia–Ukraine war and peace-talks coverage.
- Kremlin presidential archive.
- Russian official legal database.
- Russian federal budget portal.
- Accounts Chamber archive.
- UCDP/PA-X conflict resources.
- Amnesty International Ukraine reporting.
- BBC, Meduza, Bellingcat, and iStories archives.

### Unopened or access-blocked leads

- State Duma legislative records on wartime authorization, mobilization, and military procurement.
- Russian decrees concerning mobilization and occupied territories.
- Detailed UCDP/PA-X event records for 2023.
- ICRC and OHCHR reports on individual POW facilities.
- Independent investigations of Russian military procurement and Wagner financing.
- Russian-language records of ceasefire proposals and negotiation channels.
- Documentation of arms transfers to Syria, Iran, Belarus, and other partners.
- Accounts Chamber audits of military spending and procurement.
- Russian court records concerning alleged war crimes or unlawful detention.

At least 12 candidate sources were opened or attempted across UN, OHCHR, ICC, ICRC, Russian official, Carnegie, ECFR, World Bank, Bank of Russia, rights-monitoring, and media families. Several catalogue links were accessible only as archives or summaries; the strongest remaining gaps concern Russian primary legal records, military procurement, and internal negotiation documents.

## 6. Remaining research questions

1. What evidence exists of Russian diplomatic or military decisions rejecting specific ceasefire proposals in 2023?
2. Which attacks documented by OHCHR can be linked to named Russian units, commanders, or presidentially approved campaign plans?
3. What were the formal rules governing Russian treatment and transfer of POWs, and what investigations followed alleged violations?
4. How were Wagner and other proxy forces financed, armed, supervised, and disciplined?
5. What portion of the 2023 military budget was classified, and what audit evidence exists concerning procurement waste or corruption?
6. Did Russian courts, parliament, or military investigators independently investigate civilian harm or unlawful conduct?
7. What conditions did Russia attach to prisoner exchanges, grain arrangements, and other humanitarian measures?
8. How did the war alter Russia’s long-term relations with NATO, Belarus, Iran, China, and other security partners?

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

# Domestic Safety, Restraint, and Protection — Vladimir Putin, Russia, 2023

## 1. Orientation

The physical-safety nexus is established. Putin was President throughout 2023 and exercised decisive authority over federal law, security agencies, the prison system, prosecution policy, senior appointments, and national political messaging. Local police, regional officials, prosecutors, courts, and prison administrations carried out much of the conduct, so attribution is strongest where national laws, presidential policy, federal agencies, or tolerated patterns are documented.

The inherited environment included extensive political imprisonment, restrictions on protest and independent media, security-service impunity, and wartime censorship introduced after the 2022 invasion. In 2023, repression became more systematic and legally entrenched. Authorities used anti-war “false information” and “discreditation” laws, extremism and “foreign agent” designations, expanded surveillance and military-registration powers, and criminal prosecution of opposition figures.

The main domestic safety risks were arbitrary detention, torture and ill-treatment in custody, punishment-cell use, political imprisonment, forced disappearance or concealment of detainees’ whereabouts, police violence, and legal attacks on vulnerable groups. The June Wagner mutiny was the principal domestic security crisis: armed forces linked to Wagner occupied Rostov-on-Don and advanced toward Moscow before a negotiated withdrawal.

Information was difficult to obtain because independent Russian media and civil society were constrained, victims feared retaliation, access to detention facilities was limited, and authorities often treated abuse allegations as security matters. Available evidence is stronger for recurring legal and institutional patterns than for the full scale of torture, disappearances, or regional violence.

## 2. Compact evidence index

### WEB-3B-01 — Anti-war repression and criminalization of dissent

OVD-Info recorded 2,830 administrative cases under the law against “discrediting” the armed forces in 2023 and 794 people in anti-war criminal cases by December. It reported increasing prison terms, prosecutions of journalists, and extensive use of legal and extrajudicial pressure.

- Source: OVD-Info, *Repressions in Russia in 2023: Review*, 18 December 2023. [Direct link](https://ovd.info/en/repression-russia-2023-ovd-info-overview)
- Locator: Sections on anti-war administrative and criminal cases, journalists, detention conditions, and repressive legislation.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to national policy and federal enforcement; individual local acts require separate attribution.
- Contrary evidence: Authorities framed prosecutions as enforcement against disinformation, extremism, or terrorism.
- Cautions: OVD-Info’s figures are documented minimums and may undercount cases because victims and families may avoid reporting.
- Independent corroboration: Human Rights Watch, Amnesty International, Freedom House, CPJ, and Russian court records.

Supports: 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.8, 3B.10.

### WEB-3B-02 — Torture and violence in police and prison custody

Human Rights Watch reported that torture and ill-treatment by law-enforcement officials persisted. It described beatings of detainees, allegations of electric shocks and waterboarding, and failures to investigate identified police abuse. OVD-Info separately documented violence, torture allegations, denial of lawyers, censorship of letters, and abusive detention practices in 2023.

- Sources: Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2023: Russian Federation*. [Direct link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2023/country-chapters/russian-federation); OVD-Info, 2023 overview. [Direct link](https://ovd.info/en/repression-russia-2023-ovd-info-overview)
- Locator: HRW sections “Torture, Ill-Treatment in Custody, Police Accountability”; OVD-Info sections on violence and torture.
- Period: Mainly 2023, with some incidents inherited from 2022.
- Attribution: Strong for state custody and failures to investigate; weaker for direct presidential orders concerning individual abuse.
- Contrary evidence: Russian law formally prohibits abuse and courts sometimes process complaints.
- Cautions: Many incidents are difficult to verify independently; available cases are not a complete national prevalence estimate.
- Independent corroboration: UN Special Rapporteur materials, Amnesty, OVD-Info torture reports, OCCRP investigations.

Supports: 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.6, 3B.10.

### WEB-3B-03 — Navalny’s detention, punishment, and concealment of whereabouts

In 2023 Aleksei Navalny remained imprisoned under politically contested convictions. Amnesty reported prolonged solitary confinement, placement in punishment cells and a special-regime facility, and worsening detention conditions. In December, authorities failed to disclose his location for an extended period during a prison transfer, prompting concern about enforced disappearance.

- Sources: Amnesty International, “Russian Federation: Aleksei Navalny’s ill-treatment stepped up,” 13 October 2023. [Direct link](https://www.amnesty.org/en/documents/eur46/7309/2023/en/); Amnesty International, “Russia: Fears of prisoner of conscience Aleksei Navalny forcibly disappeared,” 12 December 2023. [Direct link](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/12/russia-fears-of-prisoner-of-conscience-aleksei-navalny-forcibly-disappeared/)
- Locator: Amnesty sections on punishment-cell placement, prolonged isolation, prison conditions, and undisclosed transfer.
- Period: Target period 2023; imprisonment began earlier.
- Attribution: Strong to federal courts, Federal Penitentiary Service, and national political policy; direct personal authorization by Putin is not established by these sources.
- Contrary evidence: Authorities stated that Navalny was lawfully convicted and transferred under ordinary prison procedures.
- Cautions: Amnesty characterizes charges as politically motivated; the Russian government rejects that assessment.
- Independent corroboration: UN Special Procedures, HRW, Russian court records, Navalny’s lawyers, and independent Russian media.

Supports: 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.10.

### WEB-3B-04 — Political imprisonment of Kara-Murza and other critics

Vladimir Kara-Murza received a 25-year sentence in April 2023 for treason, “false information,” and other politically contested charges. Human Rights Watch and Amnesty described the prosecution as part of a broader campaign against opposition figures and anti-war critics. The case illustrates the use of criminal law to impose severe physical restrictions on political dissent.

- Sources: Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Russia*. [Direct link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/russia); Amnesty International, *Russia: Two years after Aleksei Navalny’s arrest*. [Direct link](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/01/russia-two-years-after-aleksei-navalnys-arrest-russian-opposition-figures-suppressed-jailed-or-exiled/)
- Locator: HRW section on 2023 convictions; Amnesty sections on political prosecutions and opposition suppression.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to the federal prosecution and judicial system; presidential responsibility is policy-level rather than incident-specific.
- Contrary evidence: Authorities characterized the prosecution as legitimate national-security enforcement.
- Cautions: Human-rights organizations’ “political prisoner” classifications are evaluative, though the sentence and legal charges are publicly documented.
- Independent corroboration: UN Special Rapporteur, European Parliament, Russian court records, and Kara-Murza’s legal team.

Supports: 3B.1, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.8.

### WEB-3B-05 — Protest suppression and unlawful retaliation

Human Rights Watch documented mass detention, police brutality, and criminalization of anti-war protests after the invasion, along with continued use of assembly laws against peaceful protesters. OVD-Info reported that 2023 repression shifted toward selective criminal cases, intimidation, and self-censorship rather than the mass protest arrests seen in early 2022.

- Sources: Human Rights Watch, *Russia: War’s Supersized Repression*, 12 January 2023. [Direct link](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/01/12/russia-wars-supersized-repression); OVD-Info 2023 overview. [Direct link](https://ovd.info/en/repression-russia-2023-ovd-info-overview)
- Locator: HRW sections on freedom of assembly and police violence; OVD-Info introduction and anti-war prosecution sections.
- Period: 2023, with comparison to 2022.
- Attribution: Strong to federal assembly law, police practice, and national repression policy; regional enforcement varies.
- Contrary evidence: Authorities permitted pro-government gatherings and some lawful public activity.
- Cautions: Protest counts are affected by censorship and the disappearance of visible protest from public spaces.
- Independent corroboration: Freedom House, Amnesty, CPJ, Russian court data.

Supports: 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.6, 3B.8, 3B.9.

### WEB-3B-06 — Legal targeting of LGBTQ people

On 30 November 2023 Russia’s Supreme Court accepted the Justice Ministry’s request to designate the “international LGBT movement” as extremist. Human Rights Watch warned that the ruling heightened risks of prosecution for LGBTQ people, activists, and organizations. The decision followed broader restrictions on LGBTQ expression and gender identity.

- Source: Human Rights Watch, “Russia: Supreme Court Bans ‘LGBT Movement’ as ‘Extremist’,” 30 November 2023. [Direct link](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/11/30/russia-supreme-court-bans-lgbt-movement-extremist)
- Locator: Summary of the Supreme Court ruling and consequences for LGBTQ people and organizations.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to the federal executive and judicial system; the case was initiated by the Justice Ministry and decided by the Supreme Court.
- Contrary evidence: The government stated that the ruling targeted an alleged organization or movement, not individuals’ private identity.
- Cautions: The full practical enforcement consequences were initially uncertain; subsequent enforcement should be researched separately.
- Independent corroboration: Russian Supreme Court case materials, Amnesty, RFE/RL, Reuters, LGBTQ-rights organizations.

Supports: 3B.3, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.7, 3B.10.

### WEB-3B-07 — Expanded coercive and surveillance powers

In 2023 Russia expanded military-registration and coercive powers, including electronic summonses, a unified military-registration system, restrictions on people who failed to respond to summonses, and harsher penalties for treason, terrorism, and sabotage. These laws increased state capacity to restrict movement, property transactions, communication, and political dissent.

- Source: OVD-Info, *Repressions in Russia in 2023: Review*. [Direct link](https://data.ovd.info/repressii-v-rossii-v-2023-godu-obzor-ovd-info)
- Locator: Legislative-repression section, especially passages on electronic summonses, treason, sabotage, and expanded military-discreditation provisions.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for national legislative policy and presidential approval; local implementation varied.
- Contrary evidence: Authorities described the measures as administrative modernization and national defense.
- Cautions: OVD-Info summarizes legislation from a rights perspective; final legal analysis should use the official statutory text.
- Independent corroboration: Russian legal database, State Duma records, Kremlin decrees, HRW.

Supports: 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.8, 3B.9.

### WEB-3B-08 — Wagner mutiny as domestic security crisis

On 23–24 June 2023 Wagner forces occupied military facilities in Rostov-on-Don and advanced toward Moscow before a negotiated withdrawal. The crisis exposed risks created by the government’s reliance on a heavily armed aligned group and showed that domestic coercive authority was not fully unified. Putin avoided a prolonged armed confrontation, but the episode also revealed a serious failure of preventive control.

- Source: Carnegie Endowment, “Band-Aid Politics: How the Kremlin Is Dealing With the Fallout from Prigozhin’s Mutiny.” [Direct link](https://carnegieendowment.org/russia-eurasia/politika/2023/06/band-aid-politics-how-the-kremlin-is-dealing-with-the-fallout-from-prigozhins-mutiny)
- Locator: Sections describing Wagner’s advance, the negotiated withdrawal, and the Kremlin’s response.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for Putin’s political responsibility for the proxy-force environment and crisis response; indirect for Prigozhin’s autonomous decisions.
- Contrary evidence: The crisis ended without a battle for Moscow and federal control was restored.
- Cautions: Internal negotiations and the precise chain of command remain partly opaque.
- Independent corroboration: AP, ECFR, Congressional Research Service, Kremlin statements.

Supports: 3B.2, 3B.6, 3B.9, 3B.10.

### WEB-3B-09 — Absence of effective independent remedy

Across the documented cases, authorities maintained formal courts, prosecutors, prison oversight, and complaint procedures, but independent monitors reported repeated failures to investigate torture, police violence, politically motivated detention, and prison mistreatment. The combination of restricted media, pressure on lawyers and NGOs, and security-based legal classifications made remedy difficult.

- Sources: HRW, *World Report 2023: Russian Federation*. [Direct link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2023/country-chapters/russian-federation); OVD-Info 2023 overview. [Direct link](https://ovd.info/en/repression-russia-2023-ovd-info-overview); Amnesty Navalny report. [Direct link](https://www.amnesty.org/en/documents/eur46/7309/2023/en/)
- Locator: HRW police-accountability and detention sections; OVD-Info sections on torture, lawyers, and complaints; Amnesty section on prison punishment.
- Period: 2023, with inherited structural conditions.
- Attribution: Strong for federal legal and institutional conditions; not every local failure is attributable to Putin personally.
- Contrary evidence: Some courts and lawyers continued to process cases, and authorities occasionally opened formal investigations.
- Cautions: The evidence is substantially generated by organizations opposing state policy; official investigative files are often unavailable.
- Independent corroboration: UN Special Procedures, Russian ombudsman materials, ECtHR history, legal-case records.

Supports: 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.10.

## 3. Lens dispositions

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 3B.1 Protection from state abuse | Strong adverse record | WEB-3B-01, 02, 03, 04, 09 |
| 3B.2 Control of coercive forces | Adverse and incomplete | WEB-3B-02, 03, 07, 08, 09 |
| 3B.3 Incitement and targeted hatred | Adverse legal and official rhetoric evidence | WEB-3B-06; broader official “traditional values” rhetoric requires further extraction |
| 3B.4 Complaints and independent oversight | Strong adverse evidence | WEB-3B-02, 03, 05, 09 |
| 3B.5 Emergency and surveillance powers | Strong adverse evidence | WEB-3B-01, 05, 07 |
| 3B.6 Safety without state terror | Mixed domestic-crisis outcome, adverse repression record | WEB-3B-02, 05, 08 |
| 3B.7 Protection of vulnerable groups | LGBTQ evidence developed; women, children, migrants, and minorities incomplete | WEB-3B-06 |
| 3B.8 Safe protest and dissent | Strong adverse evidence | WEB-3B-01, 04, 05, 07 |
| 3B.9 Restrained crisis response | Mixed for Wagner crisis, adverse for censorship and coercive controls | WEB-3B-05, 07, 08 |
| 3B.10 Lasting domestic safety | No evidence of safer end-state; repression and coercive exposure deepened | WEB-3B-01, 03, 06, 08, 09 |

## 4. Information environment and limitations

Reliable evidence is strongest for:

- enacted laws and Supreme Court decisions;
- publicly documented prison sentences;
- reported numbers of administrative and criminal cases;
- cases where lawyers, courts, or independent monitors preserved records;
- recurring restrictions on assembly, media, and civil society.

Evidence is weaker for:

- the full prevalence of torture and police violence;
- deaths or disappearances in custody;
- domestic violence and violence against women;
- treatment of migrants and ethnic minorities outside prominent cases;
- regional policing not linked to federal command or policy;
- classified surveillance and security-service operations;
- whether Putin personally ordered particular abuses.

Russia’s information environment created substantial undercounting. Victims and lawyers faced retaliation, NGOs were restricted, independent media were blocked or exiled, and official statistics omitted or reclassified politically sensitive cases. Rights-monitoring organizations provide important evidence but often document minimums and cannot observe all regions or detention sites.

## 5. Source groups

### Fully opened and retained

- OVD-Info, *Repressions in Russia in 2023: Review*.
- Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2023: Russian Federation*.
- Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Russia*.
- Human Rights Watch, “Russia: War’s Supersized Repression.”
- Human Rights Watch, Supreme Court LGBTQ ruling.
- Amnesty International, Navalny ill-treatment report.
- Amnesty International, Navalny disappearance warning.
- Amnesty report on opposition repression.
- Carnegie Endowment Wagner analysis.
- Freedom House Russia report.
- Russian 2023 Foreign Policy Concept and legal materials previously opened.

### Opened mainly for corroboration

- Associated Press Wagner reporting.
- ECFR analyses.
- Congressional Research Service Wagner report.
- Russian official legal database and Supreme Court materials.
- CPJ Russia archive.
- UN Special Procedures communications.
- Amnesty annual Russia report.
- U.S. State Department human-rights reporting.

### Unopened or access-blocked leads

- UN Special Rapporteur report A/HRC/57/59.
- OHCHR report on the human-rights situation during Russian occupation and its aftermath.
- UN report on civilian detentions by Russian authorities.
- UN expert statement on torture in Russia’s war.
- OVD-Info monthly reports for June and August 2023.
- Detailed official Russian court records for Navalny, Kara-Murza, Yashin, and anti-war cases.
- Russian Ombudsman reports on prison conditions and complaints.
- Regional evidence on police violence, domestic violence, and vulnerable minorities.
- Official text and implementation records for the November 2023 LGBTQ ruling.

At least 12 candidates were opened or attempted across UN, Russian official, NGO, court, media, policy, and academic families. The principal remaining access barrier is independent verification of custody abuses and regional violence.

## 6. Remaining research questions

1. What evidence links Putin personally to the national detention, prosecution, and prison policies used against opposition figures?
2. Which federal agencies received complaints about torture or police abuse, and what remedies followed?
3. How many people were detained, prosecuted, or imprisoned for peaceful anti-war speech in 2023 after accounting for duplicate cases?
4. What did the Russian Ombudsman, Investigative Committee, and courts report about torture and deaths in custody?
5. How were security-service and prison officials appointed, rewarded, or disciplined?
6. What were the concrete effects of the Supreme Court’s LGBTQ ruling on shelters, health services, families, and public violence?
7. What domestic safety consequences followed the Wagner mutiny, including arrests, weapons control, and regional emergency measures?
8. Are there reliable 2023 data on domestic violence, hate crimes, migrant abuse, ethnic violence, and violence against women?
9. Did any national institution independently challenge or correct the government’s use of extremism and terrorism laws?
