--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 4B ---

# Political Freedom versus Authoritarian Rule — Vladimir Putin, Russia, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Putin was President throughout 2023 and remained the dominant actor in national appointments, legislation, security policy, electoral administration, media regulation, and constitutional succession. Parliament, courts, election commissions, regional governments, and political parties continued to exist, but their practical independence was sharply limited.

The inherited political system was already authoritarian, with restricted media, weakened opposition, and extensive executive influence over courts and regional institutions. Wartime conditions intensified those features. Anti-war speech was criminalized, independent observers and media faced pressure, and opposition figures were imprisoned, exiled, or excluded from political participation.

Russia held regional and local elections in September 2023, but independent monitors reported weak competition, administrative pressure, unequal media access, voter mobilization, and restrictions on observation. Putin also signed amendments restricting media coverage of the 2024 presidential election. The central democratic question for 2023 was therefore not whether voting occurred, but whether citizens could organize, receive independent information, compete, and replace incumbents.

## 2. Compact evidence index

### WEB-4B-01 — 2023 regional elections were formally held but weakly contestable

Russia held thousands of regional and local contests on 10 September 2023, including direct elections for regional heads, regional legislatures, and local bodies. Golos reported that citizens’ ability to exercise electoral rights had deteriorated compared with earlier elections and that many gubernatorial contests showed signs of controlled or collusive competition.

- Source: Golos, “Statement on the election observation results for the single voting day of September 10, 2023.” [Direct link](https://golosinfo.org/articles/146608)
- Locator: Summary of the 4,295 campaigns and provisional assessment of deteriorating electoral rights.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to the national legal framework and regional administrations; personal attribution to Putin is strongest through the centralized party and administrative system, not every local irregularity.
- Contrary evidence: Elections included opposition candidates and some contests, such as Khakassia, were more competitive.
- Cautions: Golos is an independent election-monitoring movement facing government pressure; its assessment is not a substitute for a full international observation mission.
- Independent corroboration: Golos candidate-registration and campaign reports, Freedom House, AP, TIME, and Russian election records.

Supports: 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.5, 4B.10.

### WEB-4B-02 — Municipal filter and controlled gubernatorial competition

Golos reported that candidate registration for regional-head elections was constrained by the “municipal filter,” which required signatures from municipal deputies and produced evidence of political collusion. It assessed that genuine competition existed in very few regions and that opposition participation often depended on cooperation from United Russia or regional administrations.

- Source: Golos, “Registration of Candidates for Heads of Regions in the September 2023 Elections,” 4 September 2023. [Direct link](https://golosinfo.org/en/articles/146590)
- Locator: Sections on the municipal filter, candidate admission, and controlled competition.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to the electoral-law design and regional administrative practice; Putin-level attribution is policy-level.
- Contrary evidence: The filter is formally a legal qualification mechanism and can be defended as a way to ensure local support.
- Cautions: Golos’s characterization of collusion requires comparison with official registration files and regional-level evidence.
- Independent corroboration: Golos nomination report, Russian election commissions, Free Russia Foundation electoral analysis.

Supports: 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.5.

### WEB-4B-03 — Administrative mobilization and unequal campaign access

Golos reported that incumbents and regional authorities enjoyed a major informational advantage because of access to state-controlled media and administrative resources. It described voter mobilization practices and reduced public discussion, while noting that Khakassia offered a more competitive exception.

- Source: Golos, “Election campaigning and administrative mobilization of voters for elections on single voting day on September 10, 2023.” [Direct link](https://golosinfo.org/en/articles/146597)
- Locator: Sections on administrative mobilization, authority-controlled media, and Khakassia.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to regional administrative practice and national political incentives; direct personal involvement in each case is not shown.
- Contrary evidence: Some regional campaigns had genuine competition and voters retained formal choices.
- Cautions: The report relies on observer access and public evidence, both constrained in Russia.
- Independent corroboration: Golos final assessment, Freedom House, RSF, Free Russia Foundation.

Supports: 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.3, 4B.5, 4B.6.

### WEB-4B-04 — Election observation was restricted

The OSCE/ODIHR stated that Russia did not invite its observers to the 2024 presidential election and that the organization had already been unable to observe the 2021 parliamentary elections after major restrictions imposed by Russian authorities. This is not direct observation of the 2023 regional elections, but it establishes the broader deterioration of independent election scrutiny during the relevant period.

- Source: OSCE/ODIHR, “Russian Federation flouts international commitments once again with decision not to invite OSCE observers,” 2024. [Direct link](https://odihr.osce.org/odihr/elections/russia/562065)
- Locator: Statement that 2021 and 2024 were the second consecutive national elections without OSCE observation.
- Period: Later retrospective evidence concerning the 2023 political environment.
- Attribution: Strong to Russian authorities and election policy; the source concerns national elections but is relevant to the institutional environment.
- Contrary evidence: Domestic observers and election commissions continued to operate.
- Cautions: Absence of OSCE observation does not itself prove fraud; it limits independent verification.
- Independent corroboration: Golos, OSCE election archive, Freedom House, RSF.

Supports: 4B.1, 4B.4, 4B.6, 4B.10.

### WEB-4B-05 — Restrictions on media coverage of the 2024 presidential election

In November 2023 Putin signed amendments restricting election coverage. The amendments limited access to election-commission meetings to journalists from registered media outlets, restricted photography and video at polling stations, and barred candidates from posting campaign material on blocked online resources.

- Sources: Meduza, “Putin approves amendments restricting media coverage of presidential elections,” 14 November 2023. [Direct link](https://meduza.io/en/news/2023/11/14/putin-approves-amendments-restricting-media-coverage-of-presidential-elections); RFE/RL, “Putin Signs Amendments To Presidential Election Law That Limit Reporting During Vote,” 15 November 2023. [Direct link](https://www.rferl.org/a/russia-presidential-election-amendments/32685901.html)
- Locator: Descriptions of journalist access, polling-station recording, and blocked online resources.
- Period: Target period 2023, governing the 2024 election.
- Attribution: Direct presidential signature and federal legal policy.
- Contrary evidence: The Kremlin stated that restrictions were required by the “special situation” in newly occupied territories and election security.
- Cautions: Meduza is an exiled independent outlet; RFE/RL is externally funded. The official legal text should be retained for final legal interpretation.
- Independent corroboration: Russian legal database, State Duma records, RSF, OSCE.

Supports: 4B.2, 4B.3, 4B.4, 4B.6, 4B.9.

### WEB-4B-06 — Criminalization of criticism and opposition activity

Human Rights Watch documented that Russia used “false information” and “discreditation” laws to criminalize anti-war speech and peaceful criticism. In 2023, Vladimir Kara-Murza received a 25-year sentence, while opposition activists, journalists, and civic groups faced prosecution, exclusion, or exile.

- Sources: Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2023: Russian Federation*. [Direct link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2023/country-chapters/russian-federation); Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Russia*. [Direct link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/russia)
- Locator: Freedom of expression, assembly, political imprisonment, and Kara-Murza sections.
- Period: 2023, with laws inherited from 2022.
- Attribution: Strong to federal legislation, prosecution, and judicial policy; direct presidential ordering of individual cases is not established.
- Contrary evidence: Authorities characterized the laws as necessary to counter disinformation, extremism, and threats to national security.
- Cautions: HRW’s political-motivation assessments are evaluative, though convictions and sentences are public.
- Independent corroboration: Amnesty, OVD-Info, UN Special Procedures, Freedom House, Russian court records.

Supports: 4B.2, 4B.3, 4B.4, 4B.6, 4B.9.

### WEB-4B-07 — Independent media and information access were severely constrained

Reporters Without Borders described censorship that forced many independent outlets to close, leave Russia, or change their operations. Freedom House reported restrictions on speech, independent organizations, and political activity. These controls reduced citizens’ ability to compare competing policy claims and scrutinize the government.

- Sources: RSF, Russia country profile. [Direct link](https://rsf.org/en/country/russia); Freedom House, *Russia: Freedom in the World 2023*. [Direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/russia/freedom-world/2023)
- Locator: RSF sections on censorship and independent media; Freedom House sections on civil liberties, media, and political pluralism.
- Period: 2023, with restrictions inherited from earlier years.
- Attribution: Strong to federal media, censorship, and registration policy.
- Contrary evidence: Some independent journalism continued from exile and through online channels; state authorities maintained formal media laws and licensing procedures.
- Cautions: RSF and Freedom House are advocacy-oriented evaluators; their claims should be paired with legal texts and outlet-level evidence.
- Independent corroboration: CPJ, Meduza, Carnegie, academic research on state-affiliated media.

Supports: 4B.3, 4B.6, 4B.9, 4B.10.

### WEB-4B-08 — Formal institutions remained but did not provide effective checks

Freedom House described Russia’s formal courts, parliament, and electoral bodies as operating within a political system dominated by the executive and with severely restricted political pluralism and judicial independence. The evidence does not show that courts, auditors, or legislatures regularly constrained Putin on core political issues in 2023.

- Source: Freedom House, *Russia: Freedom in the World 2023*. [Direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/russia/freedom-world/2023)
- Locator: Political rights, executive dominance, rule of law, political pluralism, and civil-liberties sections.
- Period: 2023 with inherited institutional conditions.
- Attribution: Strong to Putin’s long-term institutional system; specific appointments and decisions need separate records.
- Contrary evidence: Formal separation of powers remained, and institutions could sometimes affect implementation.
- Cautions: Freedom House provides an aggregate comparative assessment, not a case-by-case audit of every institution.
- Independent corroboration: V-Dem, BTI, Polity, Carnegie, academic institutional studies.

Supports: 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.5, 4B.10.

### WEB-4B-09 — Civic organizations and opposition networks were dismantled or stigmatized

Amnesty reported that Russian authorities targeted Navalny’s movement, Open Russia, Vesna, independent media, and civil-society organizations through “extremist,” “foreign agent,” and “undesirable organization” mechanisms. This reduced organizational capacity for opposition, monitoring, and public accountability.

- Source: Amnesty International, “Russia: Two years after Aleksei Navalny’s arrest, Russian opposition figures suppressed, jailed or exiled,” 23 January 2023. [Direct link](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/01/russia-two-years-after-aleksei-navalnys-arrest-russian-opposition-figures-suppressed-jailed-or-exiled/)
- Locator: Sections on Navalny’s organizations, Open Russia, Vesna, Memorial, opposition arrests, and civil-society repression.
- Period: 2023 with inherited measures continuing into the target year.
- Attribution: Strong to federal legal and prosecutorial policy; direct personal involvement in every designation is not documented.
- Contrary evidence: Some organizations continued abroad, and domestic activists adapted through informal networks.
- Cautions: Amnesty’s “politically motivated” classifications are evaluative; legal designations themselves are publicly verifiable.
- Independent corroboration: OVD-Info, HRW, Freedom House, RSF, Russian legal records.

Supports: 4B.2, 4B.3, 4B.5, 4B.7, 4B.10.

### WEB-4B-10 — Political inclusion and equal access were narrowed

Russia’s political restrictions disproportionately affected anti-war candidates, independent journalists, opposition activists, civic organizations, and unpopular viewpoints. The 2023 LGBTQ designation further expanded the category of people and organizations exposed to “extremist” labeling. There is insufficient evidence in this pass to assess all effects on women, ethnic minorities, migrants, and opposition regions.

- Sources: Golos 2023 election reports; HRW LGBTQ ruling; Amnesty opposition report.
- Locator: Candidate-registration and competition sections; LGBTQ legal consequences; opposition-repression sections.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for national legal conditions; regional inequality requires separate research.
- Contrary evidence: Formal voting rights remained available to many citizens and some opposition parties retained limited representation.
- Cautions: Evidence is stronger for political and ideological exclusion than for demographic groups generally.
- Independent corroboration needed: regional election data, minority-rights reports, gender-disaggregated candidacy data, and official election statistics.

Supports: 4B.1, 4B.3, 4B.7, 4B.10.

## 3. Lens dispositions

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 4B.1 Genuine electoral choice | Adverse record; formal elections continued | WEB-4B-01, 02, 03, 04 |
| 4B.2 No entrenchment of power | Strong adverse record | WEB-4B-02, 05, 06, 08, 09 |
| 4B.3 Opposition and civic freedom | Strong adverse record | WEB-4B-05, 06, 07, 09 |
| 4B.4 Independent checks on power | Adverse institutional evidence | WEB-4B-04, 05, 06, 08 |
| 4B.5 Politically neutral institutions | Adverse and incomplete | WEB-4B-02, 03, 08, 09 |
| 4B.6 Independent information and media | Strong adverse record | WEB-4B-05, 06, 07 |
| 4B.7 Equal political rights | Adverse for opposition and LGBTQ groups; broader demographic evidence incomplete | WEB-4B-09, 10 |
| 4B.8 Term limits and transfer | 2023 succession evidence incomplete; 2024-election preparation documented | WEB-4B-05, 08 |
| 4B.9 Digital freedom and surveillance | Adverse evidence on blocked resources and online controls; surveillance procurement gap | WEB-4B-05, 06, 07 |
| 4B.10 Lasting democratic resilience | No evidence of strengthening; political competition and independent scrutiny weakened | WEB-4B-01, 04, 07, 08, 09 |

## 4. Information environment and limitations

The strongest evidence concerns:

- election-monitoring assessments;
- candidate-registration rules;
- enacted media and election-law amendments;
- public court sentences;
- organization designations;
- media-blocking and censorship rules.

The main limitations are:

- OSCE/ODIHR did not independently observe Russian national elections in the relevant period;
- Golos operated under legal and political pressure;
- independent media were blocked, exiled, or designated undesirable;
- official election data were difficult to independently audit;
- digital voting and administrative mobilization reduced transparency;
- the full effect of repression on voter behavior and turnout cannot be measured reliably;
- evidence about appointments and institutional loyalty is often indirect.

The existence of formal elections and courts should therefore not be treated as proof of genuine contestability or independent checks.

## 5. Source groups

### Fully opened and retained

- Golos, 2023 election-results assessment.
- Golos, gubernatorial candidate-registration report.
- Golos, campaign and administrative-mobilization report.
- OSCE/ODIHR Russia election-observation statement.
- Meduza report on presidential-election media amendments.
- RFE/RL report on the same amendments.
- Freedom House, Russia 2023.
- RSF Russia country profile.
- Human Rights Watch Russia reports.
- Amnesty opposition-repression report.
- Carnegie and ECFR political analyses.

### Opened mainly for corroboration

- Russian official legal and Kremlin archives.
- State Duma legislative database.
- CPJ Russia archive.
- Free Russia Foundation, *Electoral Dynamics in Russia 2023*.
- OSCE Russia election archive.
- Academic work on Russian institutions and state-affiliated media.
- Russian Public Chamber civil-society report.

### Unopened or access-blocked leads

- Full 2023 Golos “Map of Violations.”
- Official election-commission results and precinct-level data.
- Russian law text for November 2023 media restrictions.
- Freedom on the Net 2023 full report.
- RSF reports on online censorship and foreign-agent laws.
- Russian Public Chamber 2023 civil-society report.
- UPR civil-society submissions.
- Regional election audit and court challenges.
- Appointment records for Central Election Commission members, prosecutors, judges, and regional executives.

At least 12 candidates were opened or attempted across election-monitoring, OSCE, rights, media-freedom, academic, policy, and official-source families. The main remaining gaps concern official precinct records, digital-voting audits, institutional appointments, and the practical operation of surveillance systems.

## 6. Remaining research questions

1. What were the official 2023 regional-election results by precinct, and what independent audits or court challenges exist?
2. Which candidates were excluded through the municipal filter, “foreign agent” rules, criminal cases, or administrative pressure?
3. How did electronic voting affect transparency and independent verification?
4. What were the appointment histories and practical independence of the Central Election Commission, Constitutional Court, Supreme Court, prosecutors, and regional election commissions?
5. What specific media outlets were blocked, designated foreign agents, or forced into exile during 2023?
6. What surveillance technologies were procured or deployed, and what judicial oversight existed?
7. How did political restrictions affect women, ethnic minorities, migrants, religious minorities, and opposition regions?
8. What formal constitutional or legal provisions governed Putin’s 2024 succession and candidacy, and how were they used in practice?
9. Did any court, parliament, auditor, or regional government materially constrain Putin’s political agenda in 2023?

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 5B ---

# Economic Well-Being and Prosperity — Vladimir Putin, Russia, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Putin was President throughout 2023 and exercised decisive authority over fiscal priorities, national development programs, state-owned enterprises, sanctions policy, senior economic appointments, and the wartime budget. The Central Bank retained substantial operational independence under Governor Elvira Nabiullina, while the federal government and presidential administration controlled fiscal policy, industrial policy, public procurement, and distributional programs.

Russia entered 2023 after the 2022 invasion, sanctions, capital controls, trade disruption, labor-force losses, and a sharp shift toward military production. The economy avoided the severe contraction anticipated by some early forecasts and recovered to approximately its pre-crisis level during 2023. The recovery was driven by public expenditure, defense demand, household consumption, credit, import substitution, and adaptation to sanctions.

The main economic trade-off was between short-term stabilization and longer-term prosperity. Inflation reached 7.4 percent at year-end, the ruble weakened, labor shortages intensified, subsidized credit and mortgages created risks, and wartime spending increased the state’s role in the economy. Household and regional outcomes were uneven, and current-price or nominal-dollar measures cannot establish real improvements in living standards.

Information quality was mixed. The Central Bank published detailed financial and monetary information, but military spending, classified budget items, sanctions effects, regional inequality, and distributional outcomes remained difficult to measure. Official GDP and income data should be triangulated with World Bank, IMF, household, regional, and independent budget-transparency evidence.

## 2. Compact evidence index

### WEB-5B-01 — Economic recovery and GDP growth

The Bank of Russia reported that Russia’s economy recovered to its pre-crisis level by mid-2023 and that GDP increased by 3.6 percent over the year. It attributed the recovery to private adaptation, macroeconomic policy, financial resilience, government support, expanding consumption, and increased public expenditure.

- Source: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*, published 2024. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: Opening remarks, pp. 6–8; summary, pp. 8–10.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for macroeconomic policy and government support; weaker for Putin personally because the Central Bank and government implemented many measures.
- Contrary evidence: The recovery was heavily supported by wartime spending and demand, and did not necessarily represent broad productivity or welfare gains.
- Cautions: Official GDP estimates may be affected by restricted data, revisions, and military-production accounting.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, IMF, Rosstat, Associated Press, and regional economic data.

Supports: 5B.1, 5B.3, 5B.9, 5B.10.

### WEB-5B-02 — Inflation exceeded the Central Bank’s target

The Bank of Russia reported that annual inflation accelerated from 2.3 percent in April to 7.5 percent in November and ended 2023 at 7.4 percent, well above the medium-term target of approximately 4 percent. It attributed pressure to strong demand, rising wages, budget expenditures, expanded lending, supply constraints, labor shortages, and ruble depreciation.

- Source: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: Summary, pp. 8–10; Governor’s opening remarks, pp. 6–8.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to the macroeconomic environment and government demand; responsibility is shared with the Central Bank, fiscal authorities, sanctions, and external trade conditions.
- Contrary evidence: The Bank raised the key rate from 7.5 to 16 percent in the second half of 2023, containing further acceleration and stabilizing the ruble.
- Cautions: Inflation averages conceal regional and household variation, especially for food, rent, and services.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, IMF, Rosstat, and independent consumer-price analysis.

Supports: 5B.3, 5B.7, 5B.9, 5B.10.

### WEB-5B-03 — Qualified central-bank leadership and delegated monetary authority

The Bank of Russia’s 2023 report records a professional monetary response: the key rate remained at 7.5 percent during the first half of 2023, then increased to 16 percent as inflation and ruble pressures intensified. The Central Bank also tightened macroprudential rules for consumer and mortgage lending, monitored financial stability, and continued supervision of financial institutions.

This provides evidence that Putin retained qualified economic professionals and allowed the Central Bank meaningful operational authority, although the broader political system constrained independent scrutiny in other economic fields.

- Source: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: Opening remarks, pp. 6–8; summary, pp. 9–11; sections on monetary policy and macroprudential regulation.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Putin appointed and retained the central-bank leadership, but day-to-day policy was institutionally delegated.
- Contrary evidence: Monetary independence was limited by wartime fiscal pressure and political priorities.
- Cautions: Central-bank reporting is official and self-evaluative; it should be compared with inflation outcomes and external assessments.
- Independent corroboration: IMF, World Bank, academic monetary-policy analysis.

Supports: 5B.2, 5B.3, 5B.7, 5B.9.

### WEB-5B-04 — Fiscal and military spending supported growth but increased structural risk

The Bank of Russia attributed part of the economic rebound to increased government expenditures. SIPRI data in the local package put Russia’s 2023 military expenditure at approximately $109 billion in constant 2024 dollars, or approximately $750 per capita. This spending supported industrial demand and employment in some sectors but also diverted resources toward war production and increased pressure on labor, inflation, and long-term civilian investment.

- Sources: SIPRI 2023 military-expenditure data in the local package; Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: SIPRI Russia 2023 expenditure observations; Bank of Russia summary and fiscal-policy sections.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to Putin’s government for defense priorities and budget direction; precise allocation decisions require budget records.
- Contrary evidence: Defense spending helped sustain output, wages, and industrial activity during sanctions.
- Cautions: SIPRI estimates and Russian fiscal data differ in coverage; classified spending reduces transparency.
- Independent corroboration: Federal Treasury, Accounts Chamber, World Bank, SIPRI, IISS.

Supports: 5B.1, 5B.6, 5B.8, 5B.9, 5B.10.

### WEB-5B-05 — Financial stabilization and consumer protection

The Bank of Russia reported measures to protect borrowers, including repayment holidays, improved loan-cost disclosures, restrictions on tied selling, stronger consumer supervision, compensation guarantees for certain pension savings, and actions against financial fraud. It also reported that complaints about misselling fell by half during 2023.

- Source: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: Summary, pp. 9–11; sections on consumer protection, financial inclusion, and supervision.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to the Central Bank and federal financial-regulatory framework; presidential attribution is indirect.
- Contrary evidence: The same report identifies high consumer indebtedness and risks from subsidized mortgages and rapid lending.
- Cautions: The decline in complaints may reflect improved conduct, reduced reporting, or changes in complaint classification.
- Independent corroboration: Central Bank complaints data, consumer-protection organizations, IMF and World Bank assessments.

Supports: 5B.3, 5B.4, 5B.7, 5B.9.

### WEB-5B-06 — Subsidized lending and mortgage policy created over-indebtedness risks

The Central Bank reported that consumer lending and subsidized mortgages expanded rapidly in 2023. It warned that lending was increasingly reaching borrowers with high debt-service burdens and that non-targeted subsidized mortgage programs contributed to rising housing prices and a widening gap between new and existing housing.

- Source: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: Summary, pp. 9–10, sections on consumer lending, mortgages, and macroprudential add-ons.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Shared: government subsidy policy and Central Bank regulation. Putin’s responsibility is strongest for the national policy framework, not each individual loan.
- Contrary evidence: Subsidized credit expanded home access and supported construction and consumption.
- Cautions: The report does not provide a complete distributional assessment of who benefited or bore the risk.
- Independent corroboration: Russian housing data, World Bank, Bank of Russia monetary-policy reports, household-debt research.

Supports: 5B.3, 5B.4, 5B.6, 5B.8, 5B.9.

### WEB-5B-07 — Sanctions adaptation preserved trade and financial functioning

The Bank of Russia reported that correspondent relationships with key partners expanded and that national currencies covered approximately two-thirds of Russian exports and imports by the end of 2023. It also reported that the financial sector remained resilient despite sanctions, while noting frozen foreign securities, payment difficulties, reduced export earnings, and ruble depreciation.

- Source: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: Opening remarks, pp. 7–8; sections on external economic conditions, payments, and financial stability.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to central-bank and government adaptation policy; outcomes were also shaped by China, India, Turkey, commodity markets, and sanctions enforcement.
- Contrary evidence: Sanctions reduced access to technology, finance, and some export markets; millions of retail investors were affected by frozen securities.
- Cautions: Russian official reporting emphasizes resilience and may understate long-term productivity and technology costs.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, IMF, TIME, AP, trade data, and sanctions research.

Supports: 5B.1, 5B.3, 5B.4, 5B.9, 5B.10.

### WEB-5B-08 — Budget transparency was limited

The International Budget Partnership’s Open Budget Survey 2023 assessed Russia’s budget transparency, public participation, and oversight as limited. The survey is relevant because major wartime allocations and classified expenditures reduced the ability of citizens, legislators, auditors, and independent analysts to evaluate fiscal priorities and distributional effects.

- Source: International Budget Partnership, *Open Budget Survey 2023: Russia*. [Direct link](https://internationalbudget.org/sites/default/files/country-surveys-pdfs/2023/open-budget-survey-russia-2023-en.pdf)
- Locator: Russia country questionnaire and scores for budget transparency, public participation, and oversight.
- Period: Survey published 2023; assesses the budget-information environment around the target period.
- Attribution: Strong to the federal budget system and government disclosure practice; direct presidential responsibility is institutional and policy-level.
- Contrary evidence: Russia published official budget, treasury, and central-bank materials.
- Cautions: The survey measures publicly available budget information, not the accuracy of every fiscal figure.
- Independent corroboration: Federal Treasury, Accounts Chamber, Federation Council budget materials, World Bank governance data.

Supports: 5B.1, 5B.5, 5B.6, 5B.7, 5B.8.

### WEB-5B-09 — Distributional and regional outcomes remain uncertain

The available record shows aggregate recovery, employment and wage pressure in some sectors, and strong defense-linked demand, but it does not establish broad or equal prosperity. Labor shortages, inflation, sanctions, regional differences, and the concentration of growth in military and state-supported sectors likely produced uneven effects. World Bank and regional data should be used alongside Russian averages.

- Sources: World Bank, *Macro Poverty Outlook for Russian Federation: April 2023*. [Direct link](https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099725104112326740); Rosstat regional indicators. [Direct link](https://rg.ru/documents/2024/03/26/document-socialno-ehkonomicheskoe-polozhenie.html)
- Locator: World Bank macro-poverty assessment; Rosstat regional socioeconomic tables.
- Period: Target period 2023, with later publication of 2023 data.
- Attribution: Outcomes reflect Putin-era policy, war and sanctions, commodity conditions, regional governance, and external adaptation.
- Contrary evidence: Official and central-bank data indicate economic recovery, rising consumption, and wage growth in some sectors.
- Cautions: Regional and household data may lag, be revised, or omit wartime distributional effects; nominal income gains are not equivalent to real welfare gains.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, Rosstat, Bank of Russia, household surveys, academic inequality research.

Supports: 5B.1, 5B.8, 5B.9, 5B.10.

### WEB-5B-10 — Public economic information was available but increasingly selective

The Bank of Russia published extensive financial and monetary information, including audited financial statements and detailed discussion of inflation, lending, exchange rates, and supervision. However, wartime secrecy, classified budget items, reduced disclosure by companies, and restricted information about defense production limited independent evaluation of the economy’s underlying structure.

- Source: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: Contents and sections on statistical activities, information policy, fiscal conditions, and financial statements.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Mixed: the Central Bank maintained substantial disclosure, while national wartime policy restricted other economic information.
- Contrary evidence: Official GDP and financial-sector information remained more detailed than in many wartime economies.
- Cautions: The report is an official institutional account and does not independently test all government macroeconomic claims.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, IMF, IBP, Rosstat, independent economists.

Supports: 5B.3, 5B.5, 5B.7, 5B.10.

## 3. Lens dispositions

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 5B.1 Broad and sustainable prosperity | Mixed short-term recovery, serious sustainability concerns | WEB-5B-01, 04, 07, 09 |
| 5B.2 Qualified economic leadership | Positive evidence for Central Bank professionalism; broader appointments incomplete | WEB-5B-03 |
| 5B.3 Macroeconomic stability | Stabilization achieved, inflation and fiscal pressures remained | WEB-5B-02, 03, 05, 07 |
| 5B.4 Fair economic rules | Financial-consumer protections documented; competition/property-rule evidence incomplete | WEB-5B-05, 06, 08 |
| 5B.5 Resistance to economic capture | Budget transparency and wartime secrecy concerns; procurement/capture evidence incomplete | WEB-5B-04, 08, 10 |
| 5B.6 Productive public investment | Defense investment documented; civilian infrastructure and productivity evidence incomplete | WEB-5B-04, 06, 08 |
| 5B.7 Evidence and correction | Central Bank corrected policy and published extensive data; national information environment selective | WEB-5B-02, 03, 05, 10 |
| 5B.8 Fair distribution | Uneven effects likely; reliable class, regional, and household incidence remains incomplete | WEB-5B-04, 06, 09 |
| 5B.9 Managing economic shocks | Strong stabilization and sanctions adaptation, with inflation and credit trade-offs | WEB-5B-02, 03, 05, 07 |
| 5B.10 Lasting economic trajectory | Short-term resilience documented; long-term productivity, distribution, and civilian-investment trajectory unresolved | WEB-5B-01, 04, 07, 09, 10 |

## 4. Information environment and limitations

The strongest evidence concerns:

- Central Bank decisions and outcomes;
- inflation, interest rates, exchange-rate pressure, and financial regulation;
- official GDP and macroeconomic figures;
- sanctions adaptation and payment systems;
- budget-transparency conditions.

The weakest areas are:

- classified military spending and procurement;
- corruption and politically connected allocation;
- real household income and consumption by region;
- poverty and inequality after inflation;
- civilian versus military investment;
- long-term productivity and technology access;
- labor-market effects of mobilization and emigration;
- whether government growth statistics fully capture wartime production.

The key analytical caution is that 2023 GDP growth and financial resilience do not by themselves establish broad prosperity. Growth was partly driven by government and defense demand, while inflation, labor shortages, fiscal pressure, and sanctions-related structural costs remained significant.

## 5. Source groups

### Fully opened and retained

- Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*.
- Bank of Russia monetary-policy archive.
- Bank of Russia *Monetary Policy Guidelines for 2023–2025*.
- World Bank *Macro Poverty Outlook for Russian Federation: April 2023*.
- World Bank Russia macroeconomic and sanctions analyses.
- International Budget Partnership, *Open Budget Survey 2023: Russia*.
- Rosstat 2023 GDP and regional socioeconomic releases.
- Accounts Chamber / Federation Council report on 2023 work.
- Associated Press economic analysis.
- World Bank global economic outlook.
- Local SIPRI military-expenditure data.
- Academic studies on income inequality and regional development.

### Opened mainly for corroboration

- IMF Russian statement and macroeconomic materials.
- Federal Treasury budget-execution database.
- TIME sanctions analysis.
- Carnegie Endowment 2023 political and economic analyses.
- Kremlin meeting with Accounts Chamber Chairman Boris Kovalchuk.
- AP and independent reporting on inflation, sanctions, and the Russian budget.

### Unopened or access-blocked leads

- Full Russian federal budget law and 2023 execution tables.
- Detailed Accounts Chamber audit findings on defense and infrastructure spending.
- Rosstat household-income and poverty microdata.
- Regional wage, price, and labor-market data.
- Procurement records for state and defense contracts.
- Anti-corruption investigations involving state-owned enterprises.
- Detailed World Bank and IMF distributional estimates.
- Independent estimates of military-sector crowd-out of civilian investment.
- Russian official data on labor shortages, mobilization, migration, and emigration.

At least 12 candidates were opened or attempted across central-bank, World Bank, IMF, Rosstat, Treasury, audit, budget-transparency, academic, policy, and media families.

## 6. Remaining research questions

1. What was the 2023 federal deficit after classified and off-budget military spending?
2. How much of GDP growth came from defense production, state procurement, and military-linked wages?
3. What happened to real median household income, poverty, and food affordability by region?
4. Did inflation disproportionately affect poorer households and pensioners?
5. Which civilian infrastructure, education, health, and technology projects were delayed or crowded out?
6. What evidence exists of procurement favoritism, state capture, or politically connected enrichment?
7. How independent were economic appointments outside the Central Bank?
8. What were the real effects of sanctions on productivity, technology access, and private investment?
9. How did mobilization, emigration, and labor shortages affect wages and regional inequality?
10. Which economic policies were corrected after evidence of failure, and which were maintained for political or military reasons?

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 6B ---

# Social Well-Being and Human Development — Vladimir Putin, Russia, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Putin was President throughout 2023 and controlled national social-policy priorities, federal budgets, senior appointments, national projects, and the legal framework for pensions, health insurance, education, family benefits, and social assistance. Delivery depended heavily on the federal ministries, the Social Fund of Russia, regional governments, municipalities, schools, and healthcare providers.

The inherited baseline included a large formal welfare state, universal or near-universal access claims in health and education, substantial regional inequality, demographic decline, uneven service quality, and social-protection reforms that increasingly targeted benefits through administrative and digital systems. The major 2023 shocks were the continuing war, sanctions, labor shortages, military mobilization, inflation, and pressure on regional budgets.

Available data show both continuing human-development strengths and important limitations. UNICEF reports that Russia’s under-five mortality rate fell to approximately 5.3 deaths per 1,000 live births in 2023. Local data show relatively high secondary-school enrollment and moderate measured inequality. However, aggregate indicators do not establish equal access, quality, affordability, or dignity. Regional variation, healthcare staffing, rural access, disability inclusion, poverty incidence, wartime displacement, and political allocation of services require further investigation.

## 2. Compact evidence index

### WEB-6B-01 — Child survival remained comparatively strong

UNICEF data report an under-five mortality rate of approximately 5.3 deaths per 1,000 live births for Russia in 2023, continuing a long downward trend from 21.6 in 1990. The indicator supports evidence of sustained improvements in child survival, though it does not measure quality of care, regional equality, disability services, or broader child well-being.

- Source: UNICEF, “Russian Federation: UNICEF Data.” [Direct link](https://data.unicef.org/country/rus/)
- Locator: Key demographic indicators and under-five mortality trend table, 2023 value.
- Period: Target period 2023, with historical trend.
- Attribution: Shared among long-term federal health policy, regional delivery, medical technology, household conditions, and inherited trends; direct Putin attribution is policy-level.
- Contrary evidence: UNICEF lists several child-health access indicators as unavailable, including care-seeking for acute respiratory infections and diarrhea treatment.
- Cautions: The mortality rate is a national average and may conceal regional or socioeconomic disparities.
- Independent corroboration: WHO, Rosstat, UNICEF SDG database, UNDP.

Supports: 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.5, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-02 — Formal welfare and social-insurance institutions continued operating

Russia maintained a national system of pensions, compulsory social insurance, family support, health insurance, and social services. The Pension and Social Fund of Russia was created at the beginning of 2023 to administer pensions, maternity and temporary-disability insurance, workplace-injury insurance, and social-protection measures.

- Source: Social Fund of Russia institutional materials and legal framework. [Annual-report archive](https://sfr.gov.ru/press_center/annual_report)
- Locator: Institutional description of the Social Fund’s functions and 2023 annual-report materials.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to federal institutional design; implementation depended on the Government, regional authorities, and the Fund.
- Contrary evidence: Administrative consolidation does not establish adequate benefit levels, uptake, or equal access.
- Cautions: The supplied catalogue’s full 2023 annual report was not accessible for extraction in this pass.
- Independent corroboration: Russian pension and social-insurance law, Government annual report, World Bank social-protection research.

Supports: 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.4, 6B.9.

### WEB-6B-03 — Health financing and service administration received additional resources

The Russian Government reported adding 295 billion rubles to compulsory health insurance financing for 2023, including an 8.5 percent increase in the payroll budget. The announcement also described provision of medical devices and mobility aids for rehabilitation.

- Source: Government of the Russian Federation, report on social measures and compulsory health insurance. [Direct link](https://government.ru/en/news/47503/)
- Locator: Sections describing the 295-billion-ruble increase and 8.5-percent payroll-budget increase.
- Period: 2023 policy and budget planning; exact execution requires additional verification.
- Attribution: Strong to federal government social-policy decisions; Putin-level attribution is through national budget and health-policy authority.
- Contrary evidence: Announced appropriations do not establish timely delivery, staffing adequacy, or regional equality.
- Cautions: This official source emphasizes policy commitments and may not document actual expenditure or outcomes.
- Independent corroboration: Ministry of Health results, Federal Compulsory Medical Insurance Fund, WHO, Accounts Chamber, regional health data.

Supports: 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.4, 6B.9.

### WEB-6B-04 — Human-development indicators show progress but not equal life chances

WHO data list Russian life expectancy at birth at approximately 73.2 years in the referenced 2021 estimate, with healthy life expectancy of approximately 64.2 years. UNICEF reports the 2023 under-five mortality rate at 5.3. Local structured data also show gross secondary enrollment of approximately 92.5 percent in 2023 and a Gini index of 33.

These indicators suggest substantial national capabilities in health and education, but they are insufficient to establish improved life chances for all groups.

- Sources: WHO, *World Health Statistics 2023*. [Direct link](https://www.who.int/docs/default-source/gho-documents/world-health-statistic-reports/2023/world-health-statistics-2023_20230519_.pdf); UNICEF Russia data. [Direct link](https://data.unicef.org/country/rus/)
- Locator: WHO Russia life-expectancy table; UNICEF under-five mortality table; local WDI enrollment and Gini observations.
- Period: Mixed: WHO estimates are baseline/context; UNICEF includes 2023; WDI data are 2023.
- Attribution: Shared among long-term policy, regional implementation, demographics, behavior, and inherited conditions.
- Contrary evidence: National averages can coexist with poor rural access, high regional mortality, unequal education quality, and financial barriers.
- Cautions: Indicators use different years and methodologies; enrollment is not learning quality, and Gini does not measure wealth or access to services.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, Rosstat, WHO European Observatory, UNICEF, UNDP.

Supports: 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.5, 6B.8, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-05 — Social policy reforms produced uneven but measurable progress

An Oxford Handbook chapter on Russian social-governance reforms found real but uneven progress over approximately two decades in social protection, pensions, poverty reduction, healthcare, and education. It describes the emergence of a more inclusive and data-driven welfare state, while emphasizing multi-level administration and uneven outcomes.

- Source: Sergey Shishkin, “Russian Governance Reforms in the Social Sphere,” *Oxford Handbook of Governance and Public Management for Social Policy*, Oxford University Press, 22 May 2023. [Direct link](https://academic.oup.com/edited-volume/46264/chapter-abstract/405493488)
- Locator: Abstract and discussion of social support, pensions, poverty reduction, healthcare, and education.
- Period: Broader Putin-era trajectory; not limited to 2023.
- Attribution: Shared among federal reforms, regional delivery, administrative modernization, and long-term socioeconomic trends.
- Contrary evidence: The chapter explicitly describes progress as uneven and does not establish that 2023 programs improved outcomes across all regions.
- Cautions: Scholarly synthesis rather than a 2023 program audit; its conclusions aggregate multiple periods and sectors.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, UNICEF, WHO, Rosstat, Social Fund, academic health and education research.

Supports: 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.4, 6B.5, 6B.9, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-06 — Wartime policy increased pressure on social services and children

The continuing war and militarization affected education, children, public messaging, and social administration. Earlier evidence in the catalogue includes AP reporting on a “military agenda” in schools and Carnegie analysis of nationalist state ideology. These materials require direct extraction before being treated as detailed evidence of 2023 service outcomes. The stronger established point is that wartime priorities competed with ordinary social-policy goals and altered the content of public education.

- Candidate sources: AP report on children and school militarization; Carnegie, *Blood and Iron*. [Direct link](https://carnegieendowment.org/russia-eurasia/research/2023/11/blood-and-iron-how-nationalist-imperialism-became-russias-state-ideology)
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Policy-level attribution to federal education and ideological priorities; school-level implementation varies.
- Contrary evidence: Government described patriotic education as civic education and national cohesion.
- Cautions: The AP candidate was not fully opened in this pass; school-level effects and resource trade-offs remain incompletely documented.
- Independent corroboration needed: Ministry of Education curricula, budgets, teacher surveys, UNICEF, UNESCO, and regional education records.

Supports: 6B.1, 6B.5, 6B.8, 6B.9.

### WEB-6B-07 — Economic recovery did not by itself establish improved social welfare

The Bank of Russia reported a 3.6 percent GDP increase in 2023, falling unemployment pressures, rising consumption, and an economy recovering to its pre-crisis level. It also reported 7.4 percent year-end inflation, labor shortages, ruble depreciation, and rapidly expanding credit. These conditions likely produced mixed effects: higher wages and employment in some sectors, but price pressure and uneven gains across households and regions.

- Source: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: Summary and Governor’s opening remarks, pp. 6–10.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Shared among government spending, central-bank policy, sanctions adaptation, wartime demand, and external conditions.
- Contrary evidence: Official data indicate recovery and improved activity in several sectors.
- Cautions: GDP, consumption, and average wages do not reveal median welfare, poverty, service quality, or regional distribution.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank Macro Poverty Outlook, Rosstat poverty data, household surveys, regional statistics.

Supports: 6B.1, 6B.3, 6B.5, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-08 — Data gaps limited independent evaluation of welfare outcomes

UNICEF provides useful child-health and education data but marks several service-access indicators unavailable. The Bank of Russia and official agencies publish extensive macroeconomic and administrative statistics, while wartime secrecy, restricted civil society, and uneven regional reporting limit independent evaluation of health, education, poverty, disability, and social-protection outcomes.

- Sources: UNICEF Russia data. [Direct link](https://data.unicef.org/country/rus/); UNICEF SDG assessment. [Direct link](https://data.unicef.org/sdgs/country/rus/); World Bank Poverty and Inequality Platform. [Direct link](https://pip.worldbank.org/country-profiles/RUS)
- Locator: UNICEF unavailable child-health access fields; SDG data table; World Bank Russia poverty-profile metadata.
- Period: Target period 2023 with varying reference years.
- Attribution: Mixed: statistical limitations reflect national data practices, international coverage gaps, and wartime conditions.
- Contrary evidence: Official agencies continue to publish demographic, education, and health indicators.
- Cautions: Missing data do not prove poor service delivery, but they reduce confidence in claims of equal access and correction.
- Independent corroboration needed: Rosstat microdata, household surveys, regional audits, WHO, UNESCO, UNICEF, and Social Fund records.

Supports: 6B.5, 6B.7, 6B.8, 6B.10.

## 3. Lens dispositions

| 6B.2 Access to essential services | Health, education, and social-insurance systems documented; effective access incomplete | WEB-6B-01, 03, 04, 05 |
| 6B.3 Priority for vulnerable groups | Child and rehabilitation measures documented; poverty, disability, older people, and regional incidence incomplete | WEB-6B-01, 03, 07 |
| 6B.4 Professional service delivery | Central social and health institutions documented; staffing and procurement outcomes incomplete | WEB-6B-02, 03, 05 |
| 6B.5 Measurement and correction | Central-bank and demographic measurement relatively strong; social-service access data incomplete | WEB-6B-01, 04, 08 |
| 6B.6 Protection during crises | Pandemic baseline and wartime/economic-shock measures present; 2023 crisis-response evidence incomplete | WEB-6B-03, 07 |
| 6B.7 No political allocation of welfare | No complete evidence on political conditionality or punishment in welfare delivery | Gap |
| 6B.8 Dignity and equal opportunity | National indicators show broad provision; disability, minority, gender, and marginalized-group remedy incomplete | WEB-6B-04, 06, 08 |
| 6B.9 Durable social institutions | Social Fund, health insurance, and long-term welfare institutions documented | WEB-6B-02, 03, 05 |
| 6B.10 Lasting life chances | Mixed: positive long-term child-health and education indicators, but distribution and sustainability unresolved | WEB-6B-01, 04, 05, 07, 08 |

## 4. Information environment and limitations

The strongest evidence concerns:

- national child mortality;
- life expectancy and health-system indicators;
- formal social-insurance institutions;
- central-government health financing;
- enrollment and inequality indicators;
- long-term social-policy reforms.

The weakest evidence concerns:

- actual access and quality by region;
- waiting times, rural staffing, medicine availability, and out-of-pocket costs;
- disability inclusion and accessibility;
- poverty incidence after inflation;
- gender, minority, migrant, and elderly outcomes;
- education quality and learning outcomes;
- political conditionality in welfare administration;
- wartime displacement and service access;
- execution rather than announcement of social budgets.

The distinction between a formal entitlement and an effective service is central. Russia’s national averages and institutional coverage are substantial, but they do not establish that disadvantaged citizens received equal, affordable, or dignified access.

## 5. Source groups

### Fully opened and retained

- UNICEF Russia country data.
- UNICEF child-related SDG assessment.
- WHO *World Health Statistics 2023*.
- World Bank Poverty and Inequality Platform.
- Oxford Handbook chapter on Russian social-governance reforms.
- Bank of Russia *Annual Report for 2023*.
- Russian Government social-financing announcement.
- World Bank Macro Poverty Outlook.
- Carnegie *Blood and Iron*.
- Human Rights Watch social and civic-rights materials previously opened.

### Opened mainly for corroboration

- UNDP *Human Development Report 2023–24*.
- World Bank Russia social-protection and poverty materials.
- World Bank Systematic Country Diagnostic.
- Federation Council / Accounts Chamber report.
- ECFR analysis of late-stage Putinism.
- Russian Government annual report for 2023.
- Rosstat GDP and regional statistics.
- Social Fund institutional and annual-report archives.

### Unopened or access-blocked leads

- UNICEF Russia detailed country data tables and child SDG metadata.
- WHO European Observatory Russia health-system profile.
- Ministry of Health 2023 results.
- Social Fund of Russia Annual Report 2023.
- Rosstat poverty data for the third quarter and first nine months of 2023.
- Federal law executing the Pension and Social Insurance Fund budget.
- Government 2023 annual report to the State Duma.
- Detailed regional health, education, disability, and housing data.
- AP report on militarization and children’s education.
- Amnesty and UN materials on human-rights effects of the war and displacement.

At least 12 candidates were opened or attempted across UNICEF, WHO, World Bank, UNDP, Russian government, Ministry of Health, Social Fund, Rosstat, academic, Carnegie, and rights-reporting families. The main remaining gap is execution and distribution: the available record is much stronger on national commitments and averages than on actual service quality and incidence.

## 6. Remaining research questions

1. What were actual 2023 health, education, and social-protection expenditures compared with appropriations?
2. How did regional inequality affect access to doctors, hospitals, medicines, schools, housing, and disability services?
3. What were poverty and real-income outcomes by household type, region, age, gender, and disability?
4. Did inflation reduce the purchasing power of pensions, child benefits, and social transfers?
5. What evidence exists of health-worker shortages, rural service gaps, and out-of-pocket medical costs?
6. How did mobilization, military casualties, emigration, and labor shortages affect families and local services?
7. Were benefits or public services conditioned on political loyalty, wartime support, or participation in state programs?
8. What measurable effects did wartime curricula and patriotic education have on children and schools?
9. Which social programs were independently audited or corrected after evidence of failure?
10. Did the 2023 Social Fund consolidation improve uptake, reduce administrative burdens, or create new exclusions?

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 7B ---

# Ruler Personal Integrity and Honesty — Vladimir Putin, Russia, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Putin was President throughout 2023 and controlled the federal executive, security services, appointments, national legislation, state media, and major economic and foreign-policy decisions. Personal-integrity evidence is difficult because Russia had weak independent oversight, restricted investigative journalism, limited asset transparency, and criminalized or stigmatized many investigations involving the president and his associates.

The strongest evidence concerns public statements, formal presidential acts, international sanctions findings, investigative ownership records, and the treatment of institutions that attempted to investigate or publicize wrongdoing. The evidence is weaker for direct personal ownership or receipt of assets. Much of the available material establishes networks, proxies, unexplained wealth, or benefits to associates rather than proving that Putin personally owned or received specific property.

In 2023, independent reporting and foreign governments continued to identify extensive assets and influence networks surrounding Putin and his close associates. At the same time, the Russian state maintained official declarations and legal institutions but restricted independent verification and did not provide a credible domestic process for investigating the president or his closest circle.

## 2. Compact evidence index

### WEB-7B-01 — Public statements and disputed factual claims

Putin’s 21 February 2023 Federal Assembly address included claims that Western governments had initiated the war, that Russia was defending itself against a strategic threat, and that Western support for Ukraine represented a broader attack on Russia. Independent fact-checking by Euronews identified disputed or false factual claims in the address, including claims about Western financial support and responsibility for the conflict.

- Sources: Putin’s official 21 February address. [Direct link](https://special.kremlin.ru/events/president/transcripts/70565); Euronews fact-check, “What’s true and what’s false in Russian President Vladimir Putin’s state of the nation speech,” 22 February 2023. [Direct link](https://www.euronews.com/2023/02/22/whats-true-and-whats-false-in-russian-president-vladimir-putins-state-of-the-nation-speech)
- Locator: Official speech sections on the war and Western policy; Euronews fact-check sections on disputed claims.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Direct: the speech was delivered by Putin and represented official presidential claims.
- Contrary evidence: Presidential speeches are political rhetoric; some claims were matters of interpretation or disputed causation rather than objectively falsifiable statements.
- Cautions: Euronews is a secondary fact-checking source and its evaluations should be compared with primary financial and diplomatic records.
- Independent corroboration: UN records, World Bank data, Congressional and European official aid records, and international legal findings.

Supports: 7B.1, 7B.2, 7B.8, 7B.10.

### WEB-7B-02 — Official declarations and limited independent verification

Russia maintained formal public-office asset and income declarations, but independent verification of Putin’s wealth and beneficial ownership was severely constrained. The absence of a verified domestic finding of personal illicit enrichment should not be treated as evidence that no such enrichment occurred; it reflects the limited jurisdiction and access of Russian oversight institutions.

- Candidate official source: Kremlin and Russian legal disclosure systems.
- Context source: Freedom House, *Russia: Freedom in the World 2023*. [Direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/russia/freedom-world/2023)
- Period: Target period 2023 and inherited institutional conditions.
- Attribution: Strong for the transparency and oversight environment; no direct finding of personal misconduct in this record.
- Contrary evidence: Formal declarations and legal restrictions on officials’ foreign accounts existed.
- Cautions: A missing or inaccessible disclosure cannot be converted into a positive finding of concealed assets.
- Independent corroboration needed: official declaration filings, beneficial-ownership data, sanctions-designation records, and court documents.

Supports: 7B.3, 7B.4, 7B.6, 7B.7.

### WEB-7B-03 — Assets and benefits surrounding Putin’s close associates

OCCRP and partner investigations have documented property, ownership, and business relationships involving people close to Putin, including childhood associates, family-linked networks, and individuals connected to state corporations. These investigations support evidence of a powerful patronage environment, but the records do not automatically prove that Putin personally owned each asset or received its proceeds.

- Sources: OCCRP, *Putin and the Proxies*. [Direct link](https://www.occrp.org/en/project/putin-and-the-proxies); OCCRP, “Businessman Handles Insider Homes.” [Direct link](https://www.occrp.org/en/investigation/russia-businessman-handles-insider-homes); OCCRP, *The Rotenberg Files*. [Direct link](https://www.occrp.org/en/project/the-rotenberg-files)
- Locator: Investigative sections on property transfers, family-linked ownership, Rotenberg relationships, and proxy assets.
- Period: Broader Putin-period evidence with continuing relevance in 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to the surrounding network and patronage environment; personal attribution to Putin is indirect unless a specific record establishes control or benefit.
- Contrary evidence: Investigative reports may identify suspicious relationships without a judicial finding of illegality.
- Cautions: OCCRP investigations rely on leaked records, property data, corporate records, and source corroboration; some ownership inferences remain probabilistic.
- Independent corroboration: ICIJ leaks, sanctions records, corporate registries, Reuters, Meduza, and court filings.

Supports: 7B.3, 7B.4, 7B.5, 7B.9, 7B.10.

### WEB-7B-04 — International sanctions and concealed wealth networks

The multinational REPO Task Force reported in 2023 that its members had blocked or frozen more than $58 billion in sanctioned Russian assets and had tracked sanctioned Russian elites, proxies, and oligarchs. This establishes extensive international action against Russian wealth networks and sanctions-evasion risks, but it does not establish that all frozen assets belonged to Putin or that every sanctioned person acted illegally.

- Source: REPO Task Force, “Russian Elites, Proxies and Oligarchs Task Force joint statement,” UK Government, 2023. [Direct link](https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/russian-elites-proxies-and-oligarchs-taskforce-statement-and-advisory/russian-elites-proxies-and-oligarchs-taskforce-joint-statement)
- Locator: Statement on blocked or frozen assets and tracking of sanctioned individuals and entities.
- Period: 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to the existence of the surrounding elite-asset network; personal Putin attribution requires individual sanctions evidence.
- Contrary evidence: Asset freezes are administrative sanctions measures, not criminal convictions or final judicial findings.
- Cautions: Governments may withhold underlying intelligence and financial records; sanctions designations use varying legal standards.
- Independent corroboration: US, UK, EU, Swiss, and REPO sanctions records; OCCRP and ICIJ investigations.

Supports: 7B.3, 7B.4, 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.9.

### WEB-7B-05 — Favoritism and conflicts involving close associates

OCCRP and Meduza reporting described business practices involving people close to senior Russian officials, including Nikolay Tokarev and Transneft-linked networks, that investigators and experts characterized as possible conflicts of interest or questionable business practices. These records support a pattern of elite access and potential favoritism but do not establish a final judicial determination against Putin personally.

- Source: OCCRP, “Pals Prosper from Proximity to Putin’s Power.” [Direct link](https://www.occrp.org/en/investigation/pals-prosper-from-proximity-to-putins-power)
- Locator: Sections on Tokarev-associated networks, Transneft-linked relationships, offshore entities, and property.
- Period: Broader Putin-period evidence, relevant to 2023 patronage conditions.
- Attribution: Strong to the broader political-business network; indirect to Putin unless a specific decision or benefit is documented.
- Contrary evidence: Business relationships and proximity do not alone prove unlawful conduct or presidential direction.
- Cautions: Investigative reporting uses leaked and corporate records; allegations should remain distinct from convictions.
- Independent corroboration: Panama Papers, corporate registries, sanctions records, Meduza, ICIJ.

Supports: 7B.3, 7B.5, 7B.9, 7B.10.

### WEB-7B-06 — Weak independent investigation and accountability

Russia’s broader political environment limited the ability of courts, journalists, civil society, auditors, and opposition investigators to examine Putin and close associates. Freedom House, Human Rights Watch, Amnesty, and OVD-Info documented repression of independent media, anti-corruption activists, opposition figures, and civil-society organizations. This restricted the possibility of domestic investigation, disclosure, correction, or recovery of assets.

- Sources: Human Rights Watch, “Russia: War’s Supersized Repression.” [Direct link](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/01/12/russia-wars-supersized-repression); Amnesty International, opposition-repression report. [Direct link](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/01/russia-two-years-after-aleksei-navalnys-arrest-russian-opposition-figures-suppressed-jailed-or-exiled/); OVD-Info, 2023 repression review. [Direct link](https://ovd.info/en/repression-russia-2023-ovd-info-overview)
- Locator: Sections on anti-corruption activists, independent media, opposition organizations, prosecutions, and civil-society restrictions.
- Period: 2023, with inherited restrictions.
- Attribution: Strong to federal legal and security policy; direct personal responsibility for every prosecution is not shown.
- Contrary evidence: Formal courts, auditors, and official investigation bodies continued to operate.
- Cautions: Rights organizations document adverse cases and may not capture closed or favorable investigations.
- Independent corroboration: Freedom House, RSF, CPJ, UN Special Procedures, Russian court records.

Supports: 7B.2, 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.10.

### WEB-7B-07 — Loyalist and patronage-based appointment environment

The available evidence indicates that Putin’s system relied heavily on long-standing personal, security-service, regional, and business relationships. OCCRP reporting identifies associates connected to Putin who benefited from state-linked business opportunities, while broader scholarship describes an executive system in which personal loyalty and network access were important to political survival.

The current record does not establish that every 2023 appointment was made for loyalty rather than competence. It supports a structural patronage hypothesis requiring appointment-by-appointment research.

- Sources: OCCRP, *Putin and the Proxies*. [Direct link](https://www.occrp.org/en/project/putin-and-the-proxies); OCCRP, *The Rotenberg Files*. [Direct link](https://www.occrp.org/en/project/the-rotenberg-files); Freedom House 2023. [Direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/russia/freedom-world/2023)
- Locator: Network, associate, and state-business relationship sections.
- Period: Broader ruler-period evidence with 2023 relevance.
- Attribution: Strong to the governing structure; specific appointment attribution remains incomplete.
- Contrary evidence: Putin also retained technically qualified officials, including central-bank leadership.
- Cautions: Network proximity is not proof of corruption or incompetence.
- Independent corroboration needed: appointment records, biographies, performance reviews, procurement outcomes, and conflict-of-interest disclosures.

Supports: 7B.5, 7B.6, 7B.9, 7B.10.

### WEB-7B-08 — Official rhetoric of legality versus accountability practice

Putin’s official documents and speeches repeatedly invoked legality, sovereignty, and state responsibility. At the same time, independent courts, international bodies, opposition investigators, and rights organizations reported severe limits on the ability to challenge official claims or investigate the conduct of the president and his associates.

The evidence therefore shows a gap between formal integrity rhetoric and the practical availability of accountability.

- Sources: Putin’s 2023 Foreign Policy Concept. [Direct link](https://rg.ru/documents/2023/03/31/prezident-ukaz229-site-dok.html); Freedom House 2023. [Direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/russia/freedom-world/2023); UN and ICC materials previously opened.
- Locator: Foreign Policy Concept sections invoking international law and responsible leadership; Freedom House rule-of-law and executive-dominance sections.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Direct for official rhetoric and policy documents; institutional for accountability conditions.
- Contrary evidence: Russia maintained formal legal procedures and occasionally allowed courts or agencies to act within state-defined boundaries.
- Cautions: A gap between rhetoric and practice is an inference from multiple sources, not a single legal finding.
- Independent corroboration: Russian legal records, UN findings, ICC materials, HRW, Amnesty, OVD-Info.

Supports: 7B.1, 7B.2, 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.8, 7B.10.

## 3. Lens dispositions

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 7B.1 Truthfulness | Adverse evidence on disputed public claims; broader record incomplete | WEB-7B-01, 08 |
| 7B.2 Correcting falsehoods and errors | Accountability and correction mechanisms weak | WEB-7B-01, 06, 08 |
| 7B.3 Conflicts of interest | Strong surrounding-network evidence; direct personal proof incomplete | WEB-7B-02, 03, 04, 05 |
| 7B.4 Personal profit from office | Allegations and proxy-asset evidence; no final personal finding established here | WEB-7B-02, 03, 04 |
| 7B.5 Nepotism and loyalist appointments | Structural patronage evidence; appointment-by-appointment verification needed | WEB-7B-03, 05, 07 |
| 7B.6 Independent investigation | Strong adverse institutional evidence | WEB-7B-04, 06, 07 |
| 7B.7 Obstruction and concealment | Strong evidence of restricted scrutiny; specific presidential obstruction records incomplete | WEB-7B-02, 04, 06, 08 |
| 7B.8 Promises and good faith | Formal rhetoric and implementation gap documented | WEB-7B-01, 08 |
| 7B.9 Favoritism and clientelism | Strong network evidence; legal findings incomplete | WEB-7B-03, 04, 05, 07 |
| 7B.10 Ethical example and public trust | Adverse institutional pattern; personal enrichment proof remains incomplete | WEB-7B-03, 06, 08 |

## 4. Information environment and limitations

The strongest evidence concerns:

- Putin’s official speeches and decrees;
- foreign sanctions and asset-freeze records;
- investigative corporate and property records;
- documented prosecutions and restrictions on investigators;
- institutional limits on independent oversight.

The weakest areas are:

- Putin’s direct beneficial ownership of assets;
- personal receipt of funds or property from associates;
- undisclosed family holdings;
- actual decision chains behind procurement and licensing;
- whether individual appointments reflected competence or loyalty;
- domestic findings from Russian courts, auditors, or prosecutors.

Investigative allegations must not be converted into established facts. The most defensible formulation is that 2023 evidence supports a powerful patronage and restricted-accountability environment around Putin, while direct proof of personal ownership or criminal enrichment requires specific asset, banking, corporate, or judicial records.

## 5. Source groups

### Fully opened and retained

- Putin’s 21 February 2023 Federal Assembly address.
- Euronews fact-check of the address.
- REPO Task Force joint statement.
- OCCRP, *Putin and the Proxies*.
- OCCRP, *The Rotenberg Files*.
- OCCRP, “Pals Prosper from Proximity to Putin’s Power.”
- OCCRP, “Businessman Handles Insider Homes.”
- Freedom House Russia 2023.
- Human Rights Watch repression materials.
- Amnesty opposition-repression materials.
- OVD-Info 2023 repression overview.
- Putin’s 2023 Foreign Policy Concept.

### Opened mainly for corroboration

- OCCRP Russian Asset Tracker.
- Meduza investigative archive.
- UK, EU, US, Swiss, and REPO sanctions records.
- Chatham House analysis of de-privatization and loyalist oligarchs.
- Russian Supreme Court and Constitutional Court materials.
- UN Special Procedures communications.
- Russian official speeches and legal archives.

### Unopened or access-blocked leads

- Putin’s 2023 asset and income declaration.
- Russian official beneficial-ownership and conflict-of-interest filings.
- OCCRP “Love, Offshores, and Administrative Resources.”
- OCCRP “Putin and the Proxies” underlying data files.
- REPO underlying asset-tracing evidence.
- GRECO anti-corruption compliance materials.
- Russian procurement records under laws 44-FZ and 223-FZ.
- State-business appointment records for 2023.
- Russian court and audit records involving Putin’s associates.
- Constitutional Court Decision No. 13-P of 31 March 2023.

At least 12 candidates were opened or attempted across official, investigative, sanctions, court, academic, and rights-monitoring source families. The main remaining gap is direct verification of personal beneficial ownership and decision-level evidence linking Putin to particular assets or transactions.

## 6. Remaining research questions

1. What did Putin’s official 2023 asset and income declaration disclose?
2. Which properties, trusts, companies, yachts, aircraft, or bank accounts can be linked to Putin through primary ownership or control records?
3. What evidence connects family members or close associates to state contracts, licenses, regulatory decisions, or privileged access in 2023?
4. Which 2023 appointments involved relatives, long-standing friends, security-service associates, or business partners?
5. Did the Russian Anti-Corruption Council, Prosecutor General, Accounts Chamber, or courts investigate any Putin-linked conflicts?
6. What specific claims in Putin’s 2023 speeches were independently proven false, corrected, or repeated after rebuttal?
7. How did the Kremlin respond to investigative reporting about assets and patronage?
8. Which foreign sanctions findings identify Putin personally rather than only associates or proxies?
9. What procurement, licensing, or de-privatization decisions in 2023 benefited politically connected groups?
10. Were any assets recovered, contracts cancelled, officials disciplined, or disclosures corrected after credible findings?
