--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 8B ---

# Ruler Effectiveness and Competence — Vladimir Putin, Russia, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Putin was President throughout 2023 and had decisive authority over Russia’s foreign policy, military strategy, security policy, senior appointments, national legislation, state budgets, and strategic programs. His system had substantial territorial and administrative reach, but implementation depended on ministries, regional governments, the military, security services, state corporations, and the Central Bank.

The principal goals visible in 2023 were:

- sustain the war against Ukraine and preserve control over occupied territories;
- prevent military defeat and maintain regime security;
- adapt the economy to sanctions and protect domestic production;
- preserve macroeconomic and financial stability;
- strengthen a state-centered, nationalist political order;
- prepare the institutional and media environment for the 2024 presidential election;
- maintain social support through wages, benefits, and targeted programs.

The record shows strong short-term mobilization capacity and resilience in finance, industrial production, sanctions adaptation, and political control. It also shows major failures or costs: the war’s failure to produce a quick victory, the Wagner mutiny, inflation, labor shortages, weak transparency, repression, and reliance on increasingly militarized and coercive institutions. Effectiveness therefore varies sharply by goal: high for regime preservation and emergency mobilization; mixed for macroeconomic stabilization; poor or unresolved for the war’s stated strategic objectives and long-term civilian development.

## 2. Compact evidence index

### WEB-8B-01 — Goals were clearly stated and institutionally codified

Putin’s 2023 Foreign Policy Concept formally identified Russia as a major power seeking a multipolar international order, protection of Russian security interests, stronger ties with non-Western partners, and resistance to Western influence. The document also emphasized sovereignty, strategic autonomy, and Russia’s role as a nuclear and global power.

- Source: Presidential Decree No. 229, *Concept of the Foreign Policy of the Russian Federation*, 31 March 2023. [Direct link](https://rg.ru/documents/2023/03/31/prezident-ukaz229-site-dok.html)
- Locator: Decree paragraphs 54–66; Concept paragraphs 85–100, 162–217.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Direct presidential act.
- Contrary evidence: The document’s claims of peaceful and lawful leadership conflicted with continued war and territorial coercion.
- Cautions: A strategy document establishes stated goals, not successful implementation.
- Independent corroboration: Putin’s Federal Assembly address, Carnegie, ECFR, official budgets, and foreign-policy actions.

Supports: 8B.1, 8B.2, 8B.7, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-02 — Government and Central Bank converted economic goals into machinery

The Bank of Russia reported concrete measures to stabilize the economy: maintaining and then raising the key rate, tightening macroprudential rules, protecting borrowers, expanding financial infrastructure, adapting payments to sanctions, and developing the digital ruble. It also reported that government expenditures and affordable lending helped the economy recover by mid-2023.

- Source: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*, published 2024. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: Opening remarks, pp. 6–8; summary, pp. 8–11; monetary-policy and financial-stability sections.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Shared: Putin’s government established the policy environment, while the Central Bank implemented monetary and regulatory measures with operational autonomy.
- Contrary evidence: Inflation reached 7.4 percent and labor shortages and credit risks intensified.
- Cautions: The report is an official institutional account and partly self-evaluative.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, IMF, Rosstat, Federal Treasury, and independent economic analysis.

Supports: 8B.2, 8B.3, 8B.4, 8B.6, 8B.8.

### WEB-8B-03 — Short-term economic stabilization was effective but costly

The Bank of Russia reported that GDP rose 3.6 percent in 2023 and that the economy recovered to its pre-crisis level by midyear. Growth was supported by government spending, consumption, fixed investment, financial resilience, and business adaptation. At the same time, inflation accelerated, the ruble weakened, staff shortages constrained output, and the recovery was heavily linked to wartime demand.

- Source: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: Opening remarks and summary, pp. 6–10.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for state-level economic management; outcome also depended on sanctions adaptation, commodity markets, private firms, and foreign partners.
- Contrary evidence: The recovery exceeded some early forecasts and preserved financial stability.
- Cautions: GDP growth does not establish sustainable civilian prosperity or efficient resource allocation.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, IMF, Rosstat, Associated Press, SIPRI.

Supports: 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.7, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-04 — Sanctions adaptation and financial continuity

The Central Bank reported that Russia expanded correspondent relationships and national-currency settlements, which covered approximately two-thirds of exports and imports by the end of 2023. The financial sector remained operational despite sanctions, although frozen securities, payment difficulties, reduced export earnings, and technology constraints remained significant.

- Source: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf)
- Locator: Opening remarks, pp. 7–8; external economic conditions and payment-system sections.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to government and Central Bank adaptation; results also relied on China, India, Turkey, and other partners.
- Contrary evidence: Adaptation increased dependence on a narrower set of partners and did not restore prewar market access.
- Cautions: Official reporting emphasizes operational success and may understate long-term productivity and technology losses.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, IMF, REPO sanctions materials, trade data, and independent sanctions analysis.

Supports: 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.7, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-05 — Military mobilization and war continuation did not produce a clear strategic success

Russia sustained large-scale military operations throughout 2023, expanded defense spending, and adapted its economy toward wartime production. However, the war did not produce the rapid victory or decisive settlement implied by earlier Russian objectives. The continuing war, high losses, sanctions, and need for further mobilization indicate a gap between strategic ambition and durable achievement.

- Sources: Carnegie, *Russia’s War in Ukraine: One Year On*. [Direct link](https://carnegieendowment.org/research/2023/02/russias-war-in-ukraine-one-year-on); Carnegie, *Rogue Power: Russia’s Wartime Foreign Policy*. [Direct link](https://carnegieendowment.org/research/2023/05/rogue-power-russias-wartime-foreign-policy?center=global); UN Commission of Inquiry. [Direct link](https://ukraine.un.org/en/223552-war-crimes-indiscriminate-attacks-infrastructure-systematic-and-widespread-torture-show)
- Locator: Carnegie sections on war aims, battlefield adaptation, and diplomatic consequences; UN findings on conduct and consequences.
- Period: Target period 2023 with inherited war from 2022.
- Attribution: Strong to Putin for continuing the war, setting strategic objectives, and controlling military policy.
- Contrary evidence: Russia retained occupied territory, expanded defense production, and avoided total military collapse.
- Cautions: Battlefield outcomes and strategic success depend on Ukrainian resistance, Western support, and external variables; moral and legal harms are recorded separately from effectiveness.
- Independent corroboration: UCDP, SIPRI, IISS, UN, ECFR, and reputable reporting.

Supports: 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.7, 8B.9, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-06 — The Wagner mutiny exposed coordination and control failure

The June 2023 Wagner mutiny revealed serious conflict within Russia’s military and security system. Wagner forces occupied Rostov-on-Don and advanced toward Moscow before a negotiated withdrawal. Putin’s government restored control and subsequently reorganized or dismantled Wagner’s domestic role, but the crisis demonstrated that the state had not maintained fully coherent command over a heavily armed aligned organization.

- Source: Carnegie, “Band-Aid Politics: How the Kremlin Is Dealing With the Fallout from Prigozhin’s Mutiny,” 2023. [Direct link](https://carnegieendowment.org/russia-eurasia/politika/2023/06/band-aid-politics-how-the-kremlin-is-dealing-with-the-fallout-from-prigozhins-mutiny)
- Locator: Sections on the mutiny, negotiated withdrawal, and post-crisis Kremlin response.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for Putin’s responsibility for the political and security environment in which Wagner operated; indirect for Prigozhin’s autonomous decisions.
- Contrary evidence: The crisis ended without a prolonged armed confrontation and federal control was reestablished.
- Cautions: Internal bargaining, command responsibility, and postmutiny personnel changes remain partly opaque.
- Independent corroboration: AP, ECFR, Congressional Research Service, Kremlin statements.

Supports: 8B.3, 8B.4, 8B.5, 8B.8, 8B.9.

### WEB-8B-07 — Repression was effectively mobilized as a regime-control instrument

OVD-Info documented 2,830 administrative cases for discrediting the armed forces and 794 people in anti-war criminal cases by December 2023. Russia also expanded electronic military summonses, treason and sabotage penalties, and restrictions on independent organizations. The repression was operationally effective at reducing visible protest and opposition coordination, though it carried major rights and institutional costs.

- Source: OVD-Info, *Repressions in Russia in 2023: Review*. [Direct link](https://ovd.info/en/repression-russia-2023-ovd-info-overview)
- Locator: Administrative and criminal case statistics; journalists; legislative-repression sections.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to national legal and security policy; implementation was distributed among police, prosecutors, courts, and regional agencies.
- Contrary evidence: Civil society and anti-war activity persisted in adapted forms, and repression did not eliminate all dissent.
- Cautions: Documented totals are minimums and may undercount cases.
- Independent corroboration: Human Rights Watch, Amnesty, Freedom House, CPJ, and Russian court records.

Supports: 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.9.

### WEB-8B-08 — Political and ideological mobilization was institutionally advanced

The 2023 Foreign Policy Concept and related state rhetoric framed Russia’s objectives around sovereignty, multipolarity, traditional values, and resistance to the West. Carnegie and ECFR describe the war as deepening nationalist and authoritarian ideology. This shows effective ideological mobilization and institutional alignment, but not necessarily effective delivery of material goals or durable international success.

- Sources: 2023 Foreign Policy Concept. [Direct link](https://rg.ru/documents/2023/03/31/prezident-ukaz229-site-dok.html); Carnegie, *Blood and Iron*. [Direct link](https://carnegieendowment.org/russia-eurasia/research/2023/11/blood-and-iron-how-nationalist-imperialism-became-russias-state-ideology); ECFR, *Late-Stage Putinism*. [Direct link](https://ecfr.eu/publication/late-stage-putinism-the-war-in-ukraine-and-russias-shifting-ideology)
- Locator: Concept sections on multipolarity, values, sovereignty, and international order; Carnegie and ECFR analysis of ideology and state practice.
- Period: Target period 2023, with broader ruler-period context.
- Attribution: Direct for presidential doctrine; implementation through state media, education, law, and security institutions.
- Contrary evidence: Ideological mobilization can substitute for policy correction and obscure failed outcomes.
- Cautions: Analytical descriptions of ideology are interpretations, not official performance audits.
- Independent corroboration: Kremlin speeches, legislation, education records, election/media restrictions.

Supports: 8B.1, 8B.2, 8B.3, 8B.6, 8B.9.

### WEB-8B-09 — Learning and correction were selective

The Central Bank demonstrated documented learning and correction: it phased out emergency regulatory easing, raised interest rates, tightened mortgage and consumer-credit rules, and adjusted financial regulation after inflation and credit risks emerged. By contrast, there is little public evidence that Putin’s military or political leadership corrected the basic strategic assumptions behind the Ukraine war or created transparent independent review of battlefield failures.

- Sources: Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*. [Direct link](https://cbr.ru/Collection/Collection/File/49164/ar_2023_e.pdf); Carnegie Wagner and wartime-policy analyses.
- Locator: Bank of Russia pp. 6–11, especially policy tightening and macroprudential measures; Carnegie discussions of military and political adaptation.
- Period: Target period 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for Central Bank correction; presidential attribution is indirect for monetary policy and stronger for military strategy.
- Contrary evidence: Russia adapted tactics, mobilization, production, and international trade even without publicly abandoning strategic aims.
- Cautions: Public evidence cannot capture classified military reviews or internal disagreement.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, IMF, battlefield analyses, Accounts Chamber, IISS, and UCDP.

Supports: 8B.4, 8B.5, 8B.8, 8B.9.

### WEB-8B-10 — End-of-period position: strong regime control, mixed state capacity, unresolved strategic outcomes

By the end of 2023 Putin had preserved personal power, maintained regime control, sustained military production, avoided economic collapse, and retained control over a large state apparatus. However, the war remained unresolved, the Wagner mutiny exposed elite and command fragmentation, inflation and labor shortages persisted, and long-term civilian and institutional costs increased. The end-state was therefore one of short-term regime effectiveness with substantial strategic and institutional fragility.

- Sources: Carnegie, *Russia Faces Three Pivotal Moments in 2023*. [Direct link](https://carnegieendowment.org/russia-eurasia/politika/2022/12/russia-faces-three-pivotal-moments-in-2023); Bank of Russia Annual Report; Carnegie Wagner analysis; Freedom House.
- Locator: Carnegie forecast and retrospective themes; Bank of Russia macroeconomic results; Wagner crisis; Freedom House institutional context.
- Period: End-of-period 2023 assessment.
- Attribution: Strong to Putin’s governing system, with external shocks and subordinate implementation affecting outcomes.
- Contrary evidence: The state maintained continuity, paid for war, preserved core financial functions, and neutralized an armed mutiny.
- Cautions: “Effectiveness” varies by goal and should not be reduced to aggregate control or GDP growth.
- Independent corroboration: World Bank, IMF, SIPRI, UCDP, UN, ECFR, Rosstat.

Supports: 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.7, 8B.8, 8B.9, 8B.10.

## 3. Lens dispositions

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 8B.1 Clear governing program | Strong evidence of explicit geopolitical, economic, ideological, and regime goals | WEB-8B-01, 08 |
| 8B.2 Turning goals into machinery | Strong evidence for laws, budgets, institutions, and policy machinery | WEB-8B-01, 02, 07, 08 |
| 8B.3 Mobilizing the ruling system | Strong regime and wartime mobilization; internal fragmentation exposed | WEB-8B-03, 04, 06, 07 |
| 8B.4 Capable implementers | Central Bank evidence positive; military and proxy-force competence mixed | WEB-8B-02, 06, 09 |
| 8B.5 Coordination and control | Strong territorial control but serious Wagner coordination failure | WEB-8B-04, 06, 07, 10 |
| 8B.6 From plans to practice | Strong implementation in economic adaptation and repression; war objectives unresolved | WEB-8B-03, 04, 05, 07 |
| 8B.7 Progress toward chosen goals | Mixed: regime and sanctions goals advanced; decisive war and long-term development goals unresolved | WEB-8B-03, 05, 08, 10 |
| 8B.8 Learning and correction | Selective: strong Central Bank correction, limited transparent strategic correction | WEB-8B-06, 09 |
| 8B.9 Managing crises and resistance | Strong regime survival and coercive response; Wagner crisis exposed weakness | WEB-8B-06, 07, 10 |
| 8B.10 Durable goal achievement | Short-term gains, long-term fragility and unresolved war | WEB-8B-05, 08, 10 |

## 4. Information environment and limitations

The strongest evidence concerns:

- presidential decrees and strategy documents;
- Central Bank actions and audited reporting;
- publicly observable repression and administrative mobilization;
- the Wagner mutiny;
- macroeconomic and financial outcomes.

The weakest areas are:

- classified military plans and battlefield assessments;
- internal government disagreement;
- national-project execution and procurement;
- regional implementation;
- military casualties and hidden fiscal costs;
- whether formal goals reflect Putin’s actual priorities or post hoc justification;
- long-term durability of economic and geopolitical gains.

Official sources establish stated goals and formal decisions, but not successful implementation. Conversely, independent analysts often measure outcomes and institutional weaknesses but cannot observe classified decisions. The most reliable assessment therefore distinguishes control, output, outcome, and durability.

## 5. Source groups

### Fully opened and retained

- Bank of Russia, *Annual Report for 2023*.
- 2023 Foreign Policy Concept.
- Carnegie, *Russia Faces Three Pivotal Moments in 2023*.
- Carnegie, *Band-Aid Politics*.
- Carnegie, *Blood and Iron*.
- Carnegie, *Rogue Power*.
- ECFR reports on late-stage Putinism and wartime foreign policy.
- OVD-Info 2023 repression review.
- Freedom House Russia 2023.
- Federation Council / Accounts Chamber report.
- Government annual report to the State Duma.
- UN and ICC materials relevant to war outcomes.

### Opened mainly for corroboration

- Russian Presidential Address to the Federal Assembly.
- Kremlin statements and transcripts.
- World Bank and IMF economic analyses.
- SIPRI military-expenditure data.
- UCDP and conflict datasets.
- Rosstat economic statistics.
- ECFR Iran and regional-power analyses.
- Russian official national-project archives.

### Unopened or access-blocked leads

- Full 2023 Government annual report and national-project execution tables.
- Federation Council budget-execution conclusion.
- Rosstat annual results and task report.
- Detailed federal and regional budget execution.
- Military procurement and defense-industrial implementation records.
- Official records of personnel changes after the Wagner mutiny.
- National-project milestones and independent audits.
- Detailed 2023 agricultural and infrastructure program reports.
- December 2023 presidential press conference and “Direct Line” transcript.

At least 12 candidates were opened or attempted across official, central-bank, audit, international, policy, and investigative source families. The principal remaining gaps concern military and national-project execution, classified resource allocation, and internal correction mechanisms.

## 6. Remaining research questions

1. Which 2023 national projects met their formal milestones, and which were delayed or abandoned?
2. What were the executed versus authorized allocations for defense, infrastructure, health, education, and regional development?
3. Which military commanders or officials were replaced after battlefield failures, and did replacements improve performance?
4. What internal reviews followed the Wagner mutiny?
5. Did Russia’s 2023 economic recovery improve long-term productivity or mainly expand military demand?
6. Which strategic war aims were revised, and were changes publicly acknowledged?
7. What evidence exists of effective coordination between federal ministries, regions, state corporations, and security institutions?
8. Did repression improve regime stability in a durable way or merely suppress visible opposition?
9. Which external partnerships produced durable capacity rather than short-term sanctions workarounds?
10. What measurable outcomes at the end of 2023 best distinguish regime control from genuine state effectiveness?

--- RESEARCH CONTINUATION ROUND 1 ---

Continuation review round 1 completed for selected themes 1B and 2B. No score assigned.

The search added independent later-2023 evidence, narrowed attribution, and preserved unresolved gaps. The evidence supports stronger documentation of Belarus deployment uncertainty, nuclear custody, later-2023 detention and civilian harm, humanitarian access, and Russia’s withdrawal from the Black Sea Initiative. It does not establish complete nuclear command-and-control transparency or comprehensive Russian dual-use safeguards.

Manifest update: the filesystem was read-only, so `research-ledger-manifest.json` could not be modified. The records below are the exact additions/dispositions to apply; no existing items were overwritten.

### Chapter 1B — Nuclear and existential-risk responsibility

New findings:

- FAS satellite analysis found no conclusive visual evidence by mid-2023 that Belarus possessed an active Russian nuclear storage facility, despite construction and training indicators.
- Putin stated on 16 June 2023 that the first nuclear weapons had been delivered to Belarus; Lukashenko made similar claims on 27 June and in December. Independent verification remained incomplete.
- Sweden’s FOI assessed that Russian custody and launch authority would remain with Russia, with the Russian 12th Main Directorate responsible for storage and maintenance.
- SIPRI documented Russia’s continuing large and modernizing arsenal at the start of 2023, but this is capability context, not evidence of safe conduct.
- Russia adopted a 2023 AI functional-safety standard and amended radiation-safety legislation. These establish formal safeguards, but no independent evidence was found that Russia had a comprehensive, transparent AI, cyber, biological, or dual-use catastrophic-risk regime.
- The IAEA’s September 2023 Ukraine report was identified but could not be fetched because the PDF endpoint returned a payment/access error. Later-2023 Zaporizhzhia staffing and safeguards evidence therefore remains incomplete.

Relevant lenses: 1B.2, 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.5, 1B.8, 1B.9.

### Chapter 2B — International peace, restraint, and lawful security

New findings:

- OHCHR’s June 2023 detention report covers 24 February 2022–23 May 2023 and records arbitrary detention and other violations, while recommending remedies. It is not a 2023-only prevalence estimate.
- OHCHR’s October 2023 report documented torture, ill-treatment, sexual violence, arbitrary detention, poor detention conditions, and Russia’s refusal to permit UN access to occupied-area detention sites.
- OHCHR’s October 2023 Groza investigation found sufficient grounds to attribute a 5 October missile strike killing 59 civilians to Russia and concluded that Russia either failed to verify the target or intentionally attacked civilians. Russia denied direct responsibility and described the gathering as a military target.
- The ICRC reported more than 1,500 POW visits since February 2022, more than 5,500 families receiving information, humanitarian assistance to over 1.9 million people with safe water, and continuing inability to access all POWs and civilian internees.
- The UN reported that Russia terminated the Black Sea Initiative in July 2023 after it had enabled safe passage of more than 32 million tonnes of food commodities. This supplies both favorable evidence of prior cooperation and adverse evidence of withdrawal and renewed maritime risk.

The principal unresolved issue remains direct evidence linking particular 2023 battlefield and detention conduct to Putin’s personal orders or superior responsibility. Institutional and policy attribution is stronger than personal attribution.

### Remaining gaps and blocker

1B remains open on:

- later-2023 IAEA Zaporizhzhia staffing, safeguards, and military-activity findings;
- a current Russia-specific command-and-control assessment beyond general force posture;
- independently verified implementation of the Belarus deployment;
- comprehensive 2023 AI, cyber, biological, and dual-use safeguards.

2B remains open on:

- direct 2023 presidential or senior-command authorization records;
- Russian detention-facility records and POW access evidence;
- superior-responsibility evidence for specific battlefield incidents;
- broader favorable diplomatic and humanitarian outcomes beyond the Black Sea Initiative and ICRC operations.

The source universe is not fully saturated because the IAEA report and UN Black Sea achievement page were access-blocked, while restricted access to Russian-controlled detention and nuclear facilities creates a substantive closed-system limitation.
